\chapter*{Preface}
These notes were written to consolidate the mathematical foundations of modern quantitative finance, with an emphasis on arbitrage pricing, stochastic calculus, and practical modeling considerations. They are not intended as a textbook, but as a structured reference bridging theory and implementation. This is more theoretically and academically focused, which I wanted to make sure I had a thorough understanding of - I would like to continue developing practical approaches to effectuate these methods. The reader ought to forgive the stumbling of an early student.

\tableofcontents

\pagenumbering{arabic}

\chapter{Introduction}
This has been my attempt to touch on most topics relevent to quantitative finance theory. I start with the mathematics of modeling assets as stochastic processes, which culminates with the Kolmogorov equations/generator framework for Markov processes that gets used for pricing. I then formalize risk-neutral pricing and how the Girsanov's theorem establishes the criteria to change to a martingale measure. In the next chapter, I price flavors of options, and discuss models for fitting implied volatility. Following that, I model rates stochastically, and introduce credit and counterparty risk for complete pricing. I then address portfolio management and risk assessment, as well as market microstructure dynamics. I round things up with time series and an overview of numerical methods for solving the proposed equations.

These notes are drawn from the following books and resources: \textit{Stochastic Calculus for Finance II} by Shreve, \textit{Arbitrage in Continuous Time} by Björk, \textit{Probability Theory} by Sinai and Koralov, \textit{Applied Stochastic Analysis} by Holmes-Cerfon, \textit{Brownian Motion} by Schilling and Partzsch, \textit{Kolmogorov Equations} by Ludvigsson, \textit{All of Statistics} by Wasserman, \textit{Convex Optimization} by Boyd and Vandenberghe, \textit{Monte Carlo Methods in Financial Engineering} by Glasserman, 1\textit{Advances in Financial Machine Learning} by de Prado, 8.S096 on MIT OCW, ECO362 from Princeton, Time Series Analysis from Baruch's MFE program, \textit{Credit Risk Modeling} by Hurd from McMaster, and, of course, LLMs.



\chapter{Mathematical Foundations}

The first half of ``quantitative finance'' is quantitative. Every pricing formula, hedging argument, and risk model rests on a mathematical engine built from three layers: a rigorous probability framework that can handle continuous randomness, a theory of stochastic processes that models how randomness evolves through time, and a stochastic calculus that lets us differentiate and integrate processes driven by that randomness. This chapter constructs those three layers in sequence.

The guiding thread is a single question: How do we write down, manipulate, and solve equations for asset prices that move randomly? By the end of this chapter, we will have the tools to formulate stochastic differential equations for asset dynamics, connect them to partial differential equations that can be solved numerically, and change the probability measure under which we evaluate expectations, which will be the central trick of derivative pricing.

\section{Probability Theory}

To model a stock price, we need to formalize what it means for a quantity to be ``random.'' Intuition alone is insufficient: continuous-time finance requires integration over uncountably many outcomes, conditional expectations that serve as ``best forecasts,'' and the ability to switch between different probability measures. The Kolmogorov axioms provide the foundation.

\subsection{Axioms of Probability}

\begin{definition}
Let $\Omega$ be a non-empty set, the \textbf{sample space} of all possible outcomes. A collection $\mathcal{F}$ of subsets of $\Omega$ is called a \textbf{$\sigma$-algebra} if:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\emptyset \in \mathcal{F}$,
    \item $A \in \mathcal{F} \implies A^c \in \mathcal{F}$,
    \item $A_1, A_2, \ldots \in \mathcal{F} \implies \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty A_n \in \mathcal{F}$.
\end{enumerate}
The elements of $\mathcal{F}$ are called \textbf{measurable sets} (or \textbf{events}), and $(\Omega, \mathcal{F})$ is a \textbf{measurable space}.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Why do we need a $\sigma$-algebra rather than just the power set of $\Omega$? In finite settings, we can indeed take $\mathcal{F} = 2^\Omega$. But when $\Omega$ is uncountable, as it will be when we model continuous price paths, there exist pathological subsets of $\mathbb{R}$ that cannot be assigned a probability in a consistent way (the Vitali construction). The $\sigma$-algebra restricts attention to ``well-behaved'' sets. In short, we are doing more work now to avoid paying the price later.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
For $A$ a collection of subsets of $\Omega$, the \textbf{$\sigma$-algebra generated by $A$}, denoted $\sigma(A)$, is the intersection of all $\sigma$-algebras containing $A$, or equivalently, the smallest $\sigma$-algebra containing $A$. The \textbf{Borel $\sigma$-algebra} on $\mathbb{R}$, written $\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})$, is the $\sigma$-algebra generated by the open subsets of $\mathbb{R}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
Let $(\Omega, \mathcal{F})$ be a measurable space. A function $\mathbb{P}: \mathcal{F} \to [0,1]$ is a \textbf{probability measure} if $\mathbb{P}(\Omega) = 1$ and $\mathbb{P}\bigl(\bigcup_n A_n\bigr) = \sum_n \mathbb{P}(A_n)$ for any countable collection of disjoint sets $A_n \in \mathcal{F}$. The triple $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$ is a \textbf{probability space}. An event with $\mathbb{P}(A)=0$ has \textbf{measure zero}; an event with $\mathbb{P}(A)=1$ holds \textbf{almost surely (a.s.)}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
A function $X: \Omega \to \mathbb{R}$ is \textbf{$\mathcal{F}$-measurable} if for every Borel set $B \in \mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})$, the preimage $X^{-1}(B) \in \mathcal{F}$. An $\mathcal{F}$-measurable function on a probability space is called a \textbf{random variable}. The $\sigma$-algebra generated by $X$ is
\begin{align}
    \sigma(X) = \{X^{-1}(B) : B \in \mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\},
\end{align}
the smallest $\sigma$-algebra making $X$ measurable.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Measurability is the probabilistic analogue of continuity in topology. Its practical importance: if $X$ is $\mathcal{F}$-measurable, then for any Borel set $B$, the probability $\mathbb{P}(X \in B)$ is well-defined. In finance, measurability with respect to a filtration (defined later) encodes which information is available at which time.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Discrete probability space]
Consider tossing a fair coin twice. The sample space is $\Omega = \{HH, HT, TH, TT\}$. Take $\mathcal{F} = 2^\Omega$ and define the uniform measure $\mathbb{P}(\{\omega\}) = 1/4$ for each $\omega \in \Omega$. Then $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$ is a probability space. For instance,
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{P}(\{HH, HT\}) = \mathbb{P}(\{HH\}) + \mathbb{P}(\{HT\}) = \frac{1}{2}.
\end{align}
Define the random variable $X(\omega) = \text{number of heads}$. Then $\sigma(X) = \bigl\{\emptyset, \{TT\}, \{HT,TH\}, \{HH\}, \ldots\bigr\}$ partitions $\Omega$ by the value of $X$.
\end{example}

\subsection{Distributions}

\begin{definition}
Let $X$ be a random variable on $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$. The \textbf{distribution measure} $\mu_X$ assigns to each Borel set $B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ the mass
\begin{align}
    \mu_X(B) = \mathbb{P}(X \in B) = \mathbb{P}(\{\omega : X(\omega) \in B\}).
\end{align}
The \textbf{cumulative distribution function (CDF)} is $F(x) = \mathbb{P}(X \leq x)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
The distribution $\mu_X$ admits a \textbf{probability density function (pdf)} $f$ if
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{P}(a \leq X \leq b) = \int_a^b f(x)\, dx.
\end{align}
If $X$ takes values in a countable set $\{x_1, x_2, \ldots\}$, the \textbf{probability mass function (pmf)} is $p_i = \mathbb{P}(X = x_i)$.
\end{definition}

The following distributions appear repeatedly in quantitative finance. We enumerate where each one arises.

\begin{example}[Gaussian]
A \textbf{Gaussian} random variable $X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$ has density
\begin{align}
    f(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(x-\mu)^2}{2\sigma^2}\Bigr).
\end{align}
The Gaussian is the default distribution in finance because of the Central Limit Theorem and because Brownian motion, the engine of continuous-time models, has Gaussian increments. Log-returns over short intervals are approximately Gaussian under many models.

Key properties: $\mathbb{E}[X] = \mu$, $\text{Var}(X) = \sigma^2$, and if $X_1 \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_1, \sigma_1^2)$ and $X_2 \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_2, \sigma_2^2)$ are independent, then $X_1 + X_2 \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_1 + \mu_2, \sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2)$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Exponential]
An \textbf{exponential} random variable $\tau \sim \text{Exp}(\lambda)$ has density
\begin{align}
    f(t) = \lambda e^{-\lambda t}, \quad t \geq 0.
\end{align}
This models waiting times: the time until the next default in a credit portfolio, or the time until the next jump. The defining property is \textbf{memorylessness}:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{P}(\tau > t + s \mid \tau > s) = \frac{e^{-\lambda(t+s)}}{e^{-\lambda s}} = e^{-\lambda t} = \mathbb{P}(\tau > t).
\end{align}
Given that no event has occurred by time $s$, the residual waiting time has the same distribution as the original, so the process ``forgets'' how long it has been waiting. This is why the exponential distribution is the natural model for default intensity in reduced-form credit models: the hazard rate $\lambda$ summarizes all information about default timing.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Poisson]
A \textbf{Poisson} random variable $N \sim \text{Pois}(\lambda)$ has pmf
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{P}(N = k) = \frac{\lambda^k}{k!}e^{-\lambda}, \quad k \in \mathbb{N}_0.
\end{align}
Setting $\lambda = r \cdot t$ counts the expected number of events (jumps, defaults, trades) in a window of length $t$ when the arrival rate is $r$. The Poisson process, constructed from these random variables, will drive the jump component of asset price models later in this chapter.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
Two $\sigma$-algebras $\mathcal{G}, \mathcal{H} \subseteq \mathcal{F}$ are \textbf{independent} if $\mathbb{P}(A \cap B) = \mathbb{P}(A)\mathbb{P}(B)$ for all $A \in \mathcal{G}$, $B \in \mathcal{H}$. Random variables $X$ and $Y$ are independent if $\sigma(X)$ and $\sigma(Y)$ are independent.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Independence is a modeling assumption, not a fact about the world. In finance, independence is routinely assumed for computational convenience (e.g., i.i.d.\ returns in the random walk model) and routinely violated in practice (e.g., volatility clustering, contagion).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Lebesgue Integration}

With random variables defined, we need a notion of ``average value.'' The Riemann integral is inadequate here: the domain of a random variable is $\Omega$, an abstract sample space with no natural partition into intervals. The Lebesgue integral solves this by partitioning the range instead.

\begin{definition}
Let $X$ be a non-negative random variable on $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$ taking values in $[a,b]$. Partition $[a,b]$ into sub-intervals via points $y_0 = a < y_1 < \cdots < y_n = b$. For each sub-interval, define $A_k = \{\omega \in \Omega : y_{k-1} \leq X(\omega) \leq y_k\}$. As the mesh of the partition tends to zero, the sum
\begin{align}
    \sum_k y_k\, \mathbb{P}(A_k) \;\longrightarrow\; \int_\Omega X(\omega)\, d\mathbb{P}(\omega)
\end{align}
converges to the \textbf{Lebesgue integral}.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Instead of asking ``how does $X$ vary as I sweep across $\Omega$?'' (which isn't really straightforward, since $\Omega$ has no geometry), we ask ``how much of $\Omega$ does $X$ assign to each slice of the real line?'' and weight accordingly. This is why we need a probability measure, which gives the size of each preimage set $A_k$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
For a general random variable, decompose $X = X^+ - X^-$ where $X^+ = \max(X,0)$ and $X^- = \max(-X,0)$. If both $\int_\Omega X^+ \, d\mathbb{P}$ and $\int_\Omega X^- \, d\mathbb{P}$ are finite, $X$ is called \textbf{integrable}, and
\begin{align}
    \int_\Omega X\, d\mathbb{P} = \int_\Omega X^+\, d\mathbb{P} - \int_\Omega X^-\, d\mathbb{P}.
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
$X$ is integrable if and only if $\int_\Omega |X|\, d\mathbb{P} < \infty$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $|X| = X^+ + X^-$ and $X^+, X^- \leq |X|$, finiteness of $\int|X|$ implies finiteness of $\int X^\pm$ by the comparison property, so $X$ is integrable. Conversely, if both parts have finite integrals, linearity gives $\int|X| = \int X^+ + \int X^- < \infty$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{expectation} of a random variable $X$ on $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$ is
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}[X] = \int_\Omega X(\omega)\, d\mathbb{P}(\omega).
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{variance} and \textbf{covariance} of integrable random variables $X, Y$ are
\begin{align}
    \text{Var}(X) &= \mathbb{E}[(X - \mathbb{E}[X])^2], \\
    \text{Cov}(X,Y) &= \mathbb{E}[(X - \mathbb{E}[X])(Y - \mathbb{E}[Y])].
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Change of variable / Law of the unconscious statistician]
Let $X$ be a random variable with distribution $\mu_X$ and $g$ a Borel-measurable function with $\int |g(x)|\, d\mu_X(x) < \infty$. Then
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}[g(X)] = \int_\Omega g(X(\omega))\, d\mathbb{P}(\omega) = \int_{\mathbb{R}} g(x)\, d\mu_X(x).
\end{align}
If $X$ has density $f$, then $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] = \int_{\mathbb{R}} g(x) f(x)\, dx$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[Standard machine]
The proof proceeds by verifying the result for indicator functions $g = \mathbb{I}_B$, extending by linearity to simple functions, then using monotone convergence to handle general non-negative measurable functions, and finally the general case by decomposition into positive and negative parts. The reader will forgive me for not belaboring the theory given the amount content requiring treatment.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Conditioning and Change of Measure}

Conditional expectation is the mathematical formalization of ``best forecast given partial information.'' Change of measure, which reweights probabilities, is the mechanism behind risk-neutral pricing.

\begin{definition}
Let $X$ be an integrable random variable on $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$ and $\mathcal{G} \subseteq \mathcal{F}$ a sub-$\sigma$-algebra. The \textbf{conditional expectation} $\mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}]$ is any random variable that is:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\mathcal{G}$-measurable, and
    \item satisfies $\int_A \mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}]\, d\mathbb{P} = \int_A X\, d\mathbb{P}$ for all $A \in \mathcal{G}$ (partial averaging).
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
The conditional expectation $\mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}]$ is the best $L^2$-approximation of $X$ among $\mathcal{G}$-measurable random variables, or the ``projection'' of $X$ onto the subspace of random variables whose values are determined by $\mathcal{G}$. In financial terms: if $\mathcal{G}$ represents information available at time $s$, then $\mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}]$ is the best estimate of the future quantity $X$ based on what is known at time $s$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
Let $\mathcal{H} \subseteq \mathcal{G} \subseteq \mathcal{F}$ be sub-$\sigma$-algebras and $X, Y$ integrable. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item If $X$ is $\mathcal{G}$-measurable: $\mathbb{E}[XY \mid \mathcal{G}] = X \cdot \mathbb{E}[Y \mid \mathcal{G}]$ (taking out what is known).
    \item $\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}] \mid \mathcal{H}] = \mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{H}]$ (tower property).
    \item If $X$ is independent of $\mathcal{G}$: $\mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}] = \mathbb{E}[X]$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
The tower property is the formal statement of iterated expectations: the forecast of a forecast is the forecast based on the coarser information.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Conditional expectation in a two-step model]
Consider two fair coin tosses with $\Omega = \{HH, HT, TH, TT\}$ and uniform measure. Let $X$ = total number of heads, and $\mathcal{G} = \sigma(\text{first toss})$, whose atoms are $\{HH, HT\}$ and $\{TH, TT\}$.

On $\{HH, HT\}$ (first toss is heads): $\mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}] = (2+1)/2 = 3/2$.

On $\{TH, TT\}$ (first toss is tails): $\mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}] = (1+0)/2 = 1/2$.

Verify partial averaging: $\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}]] = \frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{3}{2} + \frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2} = 1 = \mathbb{E}[X]$.
\end{example}

The ability to switch from one probability measure to another is the mathematical heart of derivative pricing. The following theorem provides the mechanism.

\begin{theorem}[Radon-Nikodym]
Let $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$ be a probability space. Let $Z$ be a non-negative random variable with $\mathbb{E}[Z] = 1$. Then
\begin{align}
    \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(A) = \int_A Z(\omega)\, d\mathbb{P}(\omega), \quad A \in \mathcal{F},
\end{align}
defines a new probability measure $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$. The random variable $Z$ is called the \textbf{Radon-Nikodym derivative}, written $Z = d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}/d\mathbb{P}$. Expectations transform as
\begin{align}
    \widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[X] = \mathbb{E}[XZ].
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
This theorem says that we can reweight outcomes: events that were unlikely under $\mathbb{P}$ can become likely under $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$, and vice versa, as long as the reweighting is normalized. In finance, this reweighting converts the ``real-world'' measure (where stocks earn a risk premium) into a ``risk-neutral'' measure (where discounted stocks are martingales).
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Concrete change of measure]
Suppose $\Omega = \{\omega_u, \omega_d\}$ with $\mathbb{P}(\omega_u) = 0.6$, $\mathbb{P}(\omega_d) = 0.4$. Define $Z(\omega_u) = 0.5/0.6$ and $Z(\omega_d) = 0.5/0.4$. Check: $\mathbb{E}[Z] = 0.6 \cdot \frac{0.5}{0.6} + 0.4 \cdot \frac{0.5}{0.4} = 1$. Then $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\omega_u) = 0.5$ and $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(\omega_d) = 0.5$, so the measure change has turned an asymmetric world into a fair one.
\end{example}

\subsection{Inference}

The mathematical framework connects to data through limit theorems that justify using sample averages as estimators and that underpin Monte Carlo simulation.

\begin{definition}
A sequence $\{X_n\}$ of random variables is \textbf{independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.)} if all variables are mutually independent and share a common distribution.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
A sequence $\{X_n\}$ \textbf{converges in probability} to $X$ if 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n \to \infty} \mathbb{P}(|X_n - X| > \epsilon) = 0
\end{align} for all $\epsilon > 0$. It \textbf{converges in distribution} to $X$ if 
\begin{align}
    \lim_{n \to \infty} F_{X_n}(x) = F_X(x)
\end{align} at all continuity points of $F_X$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Weak Law of Large Numbers]
Let $\{X_n\}$ be i.i.d.\ with $\mathbb{E}[X_n] = \mu$. Then the sample mean $\bar{X}_n = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i$ converges in probability to $\mu$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
This is the theoretical foundation of Monte Carlo simulation. To price a derivative by simulation, we draw $M$ independent realizations of the payoff and average them. The law of large numbers guarantees that this average converges to the true expected payoff as $M \to \infty$.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Central Limit Theorem]
Let $\{X_n\}$ be i.i.d.\ with $\mathbb{E}[X_n] = \mu$ and $\text{Var}(X_n) = \sigma^2 < \infty$. Then
\begin{align}
    Z_n = \frac{\sqrt{n}(\bar{X}_n - \mu)}{\sigma} \xrightarrow{d} \mathcal{N}(0,1).
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
The CLT quantifies the rate at which Monte Carlo estimates converge: the standard error of the sample mean is $\sigma/\sqrt{n}$. Halving the error requires quadrupling the number of simulations. This $\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})$ rate is dimension-independent, which is why Monte Carlo is competitive in high-dimensional problems where grid-based methods suffer from the curse of dimensionality.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Monte Carlo error budget]
Suppose we estimate a European option price by averaging $M = 10{,}000$ simulated payoffs, and the sample standard deviation of the discounted payoffs is $\hat{\sigma} = 5.00$. The 95\% confidence interval for the true price is
\begin{align}
    \bar{V} \pm 1.96 \cdot \frac{\hat{\sigma}}{\sqrt{M}} = \bar{V} \pm 1.96 \cdot \frac{5.00}{\sqrt{10{,}000}} = \bar{V} \pm 0.098.
\end{align}
To tighten this to $\pm 0.01$, we would need roughly $M \approx 10^6$ simulations, or a variance reduction technique.
\end{example}


\section{Stochastic Processes}

A stock price is a family of random variables indexed by time, each representing the price at a given instant. 

\subsection{Foundations of Stochastic Processes}

\begin{definition}
Let $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$ be a probability space. A \textbf{stochastic process} is a family of random variables $\{X(t)\}_{t \in T}$, where $T$ is a parameter set (typically $[0,\infty)$ or $\mathbb{N}$) and each $X(t): \Omega \to \mathbb{R}^d$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
There are two complementary viewpoints. Fix $\omega \in \Omega$: the mapping $t \mapsto X(t, \omega)$ is a \textbf{sample path} (or realization), or one possible trajectory of the stock price. Fix $t$: $X(t)$ is a random variable describing the distribution of the process at that instant.
\end{remark}

To place a probability measure on the space of all paths, we need to specify the process through its finite-dimensional distributions, which are the joint distributions of any finite collection of time points.

\begin{definition}
Let $X(t)$ be a stochastic process. For any finite set of times $\{t_1, \ldots, t_k\} \subset T$, the \textbf{finite-dimensional distribution} is the push-forward measure
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{P}^X_{t_1,\ldots,t_k}(A) = \mathbb{P}\bigl((X(t_1), \ldots, X(t_k)) \in A\bigr), \quad A \in \mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{dk}).
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    If we have a given stochastic process, we can define a new probability measure that evaluates based on the already-defined stochastic process' probability rules. A natural next step is if one can deduce an underlying process that could generate all of the finite-dimensional distributions.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Kolmogorov Extension]
Let $\{\mathbb{P}_{t_1,\ldots,t_k}\}$ be a family of finite-dimensional probability measures satisfying the \textbf{consistency conditions}:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \emph{Permutation invariance:} reordering time indices permutes the coordinates of the measure accordingly.
    \item \emph{Marginal consistency:} $\mathbb{P}_{t_1,\ldots,t_k}(A \times \mathbb{R}) = \mathbb{P}_{t_1,\ldots,t_{k-1}}(A)$ for Borel $A$.
\end{enumerate}
Then there exists a probability measure on the space of all paths whose finite-dimensional projections agree with the given family.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
This theorem is the existence guarantee for processes defined by their distributional properties. We will use it to assert that Brownian motion exists: we specify that increments are independent and Gaussian, verify consistency, and conclude that a process with these properties exists on some probability space.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Filtrations and Martingales}

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{filtration} $\{\mathcal{F}(t)\}_{t \in T}$ is an increasing family of $\sigma$-algebras: $s \leq t \implies \mathcal{F}(s) \subseteq \mathcal{F}(t)$. A process $X(t)$ is \textbf{adapted} to $\{\mathcal{F}(t)\}$ if $X(t)$ is $\mathcal{F}(t)$-measurable for every $t$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
The filtration encodes the flow of information over time. $\mathcal{F}(t)$ contains all events that can be determined by observing the process up to time $t$. Adaptedness formalizes causality: the value of an adapted process at time $t$ depends only on information available at time $t$, not on the future. Every trading strategy, every hedging decision, every stopping rule must be adapted, because you can't peak at things that haven't happened.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
A random variable $\tau: \Omega \to T$ is a \textbf{stopping time} with respect to $\{\mathcal{F}(t)\}$ if $\{\tau \leq t\} \in \mathcal{F}(t)$ for all $t \in T$. A \textbf{stopped process} is $X(t \wedge \tau)$, where $\wedge$ denotes the minimum.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
The definition requires that the decision ``has $\tau$ occurred yet?'' can be answered at each time $t$ using only current information. Examples of stopping times: the first time a stock hits a barrier, the first time a portfolio's drawdown exceeds a threshold, the exercise time of an American option. Examples of non-stopping times include ``the time at which the stock reaches its maximum'' - you cannot know in real time whether the current price is the eventual peak.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
An adapted, integrable process $M(t)$ is a \textbf{martingale} if, for all $s \leq t$,
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}[M(t) \mid \mathcal{F}(s)] = M(s).
\end{align}
A \textbf{submartingale} has $\mathbb{E}[M(t) \mid \mathcal{F}(s)] \geq M(s)$; a \textbf{supermartingale} has $\leq$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
A martingale is a process whose best forecast of its future value, given all current information, is its current value.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
A stopped martingale is still a martingale.
\end{proposition}

\begin{theorem}[Doob's Optional Stopping]
If $M(t)$ is a martingale and $\tau$ is an almost surely bounded stopping time with $\mathbb{E}[|M(\tau)|] < \infty$, then
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}[M(\tau)] = \mathbb{E}[M(0)].
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
In words: no stopping rule can extract a profit on average from a fair game. This underpins the impossibility of ``beating'' a martingale by clever timing, and is invoked in proving that risk-neutral prices are unique and that hedging strategies replicate payoffs.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Gambler's ruin as a martingale application]
A gambler starts with \$50 and bets \$1 per round on a fair coin flip. Wealth $W_n$ is a martingale. Doob's theorem implies $\mathbb{E}[W_\tau] = 50$ for any bounded stopping time $\tau$. If the gambler stops upon reaching \$100 or \$0, and the stopping time is almost surely finite (which it is for symmetric random walks), then $100 \cdot \mathbb{P}(\text{reach 100}) + 0 \cdot \mathbb{P}(\text{ruin}) = 50$, giving $\mathbb{P}(\text{reach 100}) = 1/2$. The same logic, applied to discounted derivative payoffs, yields pricing formulas.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
Let $Z$ be an a.s.\ positive random variable with $\mathbb{E}[Z] = 1$, and let $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$ be the associated change of measure. The \textbf{Radon-Nikodym derivative process} is
\begin{align}
    Z(t) = \mathbb{E}[Z \mid \mathcal{F}(t)].
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
$Z(t)$ is a martingale under $\mathbb{P}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By the tower property: $\mathbb{E}[Z(t) \mid \mathcal{F}(s)] = \mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[Z \mid \mathcal{F}(t)] \mid \mathcal{F}(s)] = \mathbb{E}[Z \mid \mathcal{F}(s)] = Z(s)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
Let $Z(t)$ be a Radon-Nikodym derivative process and $Y$ an $\mathcal{F}(t)$-measurable random variable. Then:
\begin{align}
    \widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[Y] &= \mathbb{E}[Y Z(t)], \\
    \widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[Y \mid \mathcal{F}(s)] &= \frac{1}{Z(s)}\mathbb{E}[Y Z(t) \mid \mathcal{F}(s)].
\end{align}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For the first identity: $\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[Y] = \mathbb{E}[YZ] = \mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[YZ \mid \mathcal{F}(t)]] = \mathbb{E}[Y \mathbb{E}[Z \mid \mathcal{F}(t)]] = \mathbb{E}[YZ(t)]$, where the third equality uses that $Y$ is $\mathcal{F}(t)$-measurable.

For the second: let $A \in \mathcal{F}(s)$. Then $\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbb{I}_A \cdot \frac{1}{Z(s)}\mathbb{E}[YZ(t) \mid \mathcal{F}(s)]] = \mathbb{E}[\mathbb{I}_A Y Z(t)] = \widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbb{I}_A Y]$. Since the candidate is $\mathcal{F}(s)$-measurable and satisfies partial averaging, it is the conditional expectation.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Markov Processes}

Markov processes are the tractable workhorses of quantitative finance: their future evolution depends on the past only through the current state. This ``memoryless'' property is what makes PDEs and generators available for pricing.

\begin{definition}
A discrete-time process $\{X(n)\}_{n \in \mathbb{N}}$ on a countable state space $S$ is a \textbf{Markov chain} if
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{P}(X(n+1) = x_j \mid X(n), X(n-1), \ldots) = \mathbb{P}(X(n+1) = x_j \mid X(n) = x_i).
\end{align}
If the right-hand side does not depend on $n$, the chain is \textbf{time-homogeneous} with \textbf{transition matrix} $P = (p_{ij})$, and $\mathbb{P}(X(n) = x_j \mid X(0) = x_i) = (P^n)_{ij}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
A continuous-time process $X(t)$ taking values in $\mathbb{R}^d$ is a \textbf{Markov process} if, for all bounded Borel-measurable $f$ and $s \leq t$,
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}[f(X(t)) \mid \mathcal{F}(s)] = \mathbb{E}[f(X(t)) \mid X(s)].
\end{align}
The \textbf{transition probabilities} are $p(s, t, x, A) = \mathbb{P}(X(t) \in A \mid X(s) = x)$.
\end{definition}


\subsection{Brownian Motion}

Brownian motion is the canonical noise process of continuous-time finance. Its mathematical elegance and tractability make it the default model for randomness in asset prices.

\begin{theorem}[Existence of Brownian motion]
There exists a stochastic process $W(t)$, called \textbf{Brownian motion} (or the \textbf{Wiener process}), satisfying:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $W(0) = 0$ a.s.,
    \item $W(t)$ has continuous sample paths a.s.,
    \item $W(t)$ has independent increments,
    \item $W(t) - W(s) \sim \mathcal{N}(0, t-s)$ for $s < t$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[Proof idea]
Existence follows from the Kolmogorov extension theorem: the specified finite-dimensional distributions (multivariate Gaussian with the given covariance structure) satisfy the consistency conditions. Path continuity requires a separate argument (the Kolmogorov continuity criterion).
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{filtration for Brownian motion} $\{\mathcal{F}(t)\}$ is a filtration such that $W(u) - W(t)$ is independent of $\mathcal{F}(t)$ for all $u > t$. The \textbf{natural filtration} $\mathcal{F}(t) = \sigma(W(s) : s \leq t)$ is the smallest such filtration.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
Brownian motion is a martingale.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$\mathbb{E}[W(t) \mid \mathcal{F}(s)] = \mathbb{E}[(W(t)-W(s)) + W(s) \mid \mathcal{F}(s)] = 0 + W(s)$, using independence of the increment and the $\mathcal{F}(s)$-measurability of $W(s)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
Brownian motion is a Markov process.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Quadratic variation]
Brownian motion accumulates quadratic variation at rate one:
\begin{align}
    [W,W](T) = \lim_{\|\Pi\| \to 0} \sum_j [W(t_{j+1}) - W(t_j)]^2 = T.
\end{align}
For $d$-dimensional Brownian motion, $[W_i, W_j](t) = \delta_{ij} t$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
Quadratic variation is the property that distinguishes stochastic calculus from ordinary calculus. A smooth function has zero quadratic variation, so the $(dt)^2$ and higher-order terms in Taylor expansions vanish. For Brownian motion, $(dW)^2 = dt$ does not vanish, producing the extra second-order term in It\^o's lemma.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[L\'evy's characterization]
Let $M(t)$ be a continuous martingale with $M(0)=0$ and $[M,M](t) = t$. Then $M(t)$ is a Brownian motion. In the multidimensional case, the condition becomes $[M_i, M_j](t) = \delta_{ij} t$.
\end{theorem}


\begin{example}[Scaling property]
For any $a > 0$, the process $\widetilde{W}(t) = \frac{1}{a}W(a^2 t)$ is also a Brownian motion. Verify via L\'evy:

\emph{Martingale:} The natural filtration of $\widetilde{W}$ is $\mathcal{F}(s) = \sigma(W(u), u \leq a^2s)$. Then $\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{W}(t) \mid \mathcal{F}(s)] = \frac{1}{a}\mathbb{E}[W(a^2t) \mid \sigma(W(u), u \leq a^2s)] = \frac{1}{a}W(a^2s) = \widetilde{W}(s)$.

\emph{Quadratic variation:} $[\widetilde{W}, \widetilde{W}](t) = \frac{1}{a^2}[W,W](a^2t) = \frac{1}{a^2} \cdot a^2 t = t$.

This scaling property is the mathematical origin of the \textbf{square-root-of-time rule}: if daily volatility is $\sigma_{\text{daily}}$, then $T$-day volatility is $\sigma_{\text{daily}}\sqrt{T}$. In particular, the industry convention for annualizing daily volatility is
\begin{align}
    \sigma_{\text{annual}} \approx \sqrt{252}\, \sigma_{\text{daily}},
\end{align}
where 252 is the approximate number of trading days in a year.
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}[Reflection principle]
Let $M(t) = \max_{0 \leq s \leq t} W(s)$ and $\tau_m = \inf\{t : W(t) = m\}$. Then:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{P}(M(t) \geq a) &= 2\,\mathbb{P}(W(t) \geq a), \\
    \mathbb{P}(\tau_m \leq t,\, W(t) \leq w) &= \mathbb{P}(W(t) \geq 2m - w).
\end{align}
\end{proposition}



\section{Stochastic Calculus}\label{sec:stoch-calc}

Brownian motion is almost surely nowhere differentiable and has infinite first-order variation on every interval, so neither the Riemann--Stieltjes integral nor the classical chain rule survives contact with it. This section builds a calculus that does, culminating in the connection between stochastic differential equations and partial differential equations that makes derivative pricing computationally tractable.

\subsection{It\^o Calculus}

\begin{definition}[It\^o integral]
Let $W(t)$ be Brownian motion with filtration $\{\mathcal{F}(t)\}$ on $[0,T]$, and let $\Delta(t)$ be an adapted process with $\mathbb{E}\!\left[\int_0^T \Delta(t)^2\, dt\right] < \infty$. The \textbf{It\^o integral} is the $L^2$-limit of left-endpoint Riemann sums,
\begin{align}
    I(T) = \int_0^T \Delta(t)\, dW(t) \coloneqq \lim_{\|\Pi\| \to 0} \sum_j \Delta(t_j)\bigl[W(t_{j+1}) - W(t_j)\bigr],
\end{align}
the limit being taken first for simple adapted integrands and then extended by the isometry below.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[It\^o vs Stratonovich]
In physics, the Stratonovich integral is another formulation of this stochastic integral, which uses the midpoint value of the process over the sub-interval, whereas the It\^o integrand is evaluated at the \emph{left} endpoint $t_j$. This is forced by adaptedness, since $\Delta(t_j)$ must be known before the increment $W(t_{j+1})-W(t_j)$ is revealed. Financially, it is the statement that a position must be chosen before the price moves. Moreover, the limit is in $L^2$, not pathwise (this is also true of Stratonovich). Because Brownian motion has infinite total variation, no pathwise Riemann--Stieltjes construction exists. The chain rule applies for Stratonovich, but It\^o requires an innovation.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Properties of the It\^o integral]
Let $I(t) = \int_0^t \Delta(u)\, dW(u)$ with $\Delta$ satisfying the square-integrability condition above. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep]
    \item \textbf{Linearity}: $\int_0^t (a\Delta_1 + b\Delta_2)\,dW = a\int_0^t \Delta_1\,dW + b\int_0^t\Delta_2\,dW$.
    \item \textbf{Martingale}: $I(t)$ is a martingale; in particular $\mathbb{E}[I(t)] = 0$.
    \item \textbf{Regularity}: $I(t)$ has continuous paths and is $\mathcal{F}(t)$-adapted.
    \item \textbf{Quadratic variation}: $[I,I](t) = \int_0^t \Delta(u)^2\, du$.
    \item \textbf{It\^o isometry}: $\mathbb{E}\!\left[I(t)^2\right] = \mathbb{E}\!\left[\int_0^t \Delta(u)^2\, du\right]$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[Local Martingales]\label{rmk:local-mart}
The martingale property requires the \emph{expectation} condition $\mathbb{E}\!\left[\int_0^T\Delta^2 dt\right]<\infty$. Under only the pathwise condition $\int_0^T \Delta(t)^2\,dt < \infty$ almost surely, the integral still exists but is a \textbf{local martingale}: there is a sequence of stopping times $\tau_n\uparrow\infty$ with each stopped process $I(t\wedge\tau_n)$ a true martingale, yet $I$ itself need not be one, and $\mathbb{E}[I(t)]=0$ can fail.

A non-negative local martingale is always a \emph{supermartingale}, so $\mathbb{E}[I(t)] \leq I(0)$, with strict inequality possible. In finance, this is the mathematics of a \textbf{bubble}: if the discounted price process is a strict local martingale rather than a true martingale, then
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}\!\left[e^{-rT}S(T)\right] < S(0),
\end{align}
so the asset trades \emph{above} the expected discounted value of its own future price. The standard example is the CEV process $dS = \sigma S^{\beta}\,dW$ with $\beta > 1$, whose discounted price is a strict local martingale, where $\beta>1$ means volatility rising with price. Whenever a model's diffusion coefficient grows superlinearly, the martingale property must be checked rather than assumed.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[It\^o process]
An \textbf{It\^o process} $X(t)$ with deterministic initial condition $X(0)$ is
\begin{align}
    X(t) = X(0) + \int_0^t \Theta(u)\, du + \int_0^t \Delta(u)\, dW(u),
\end{align}
written in differential notation as
\begin{align}
    dX(t) = \Theta(t)\, dt + \Delta(t)\, dW(t),
\end{align}
where $\Theta$ is the \textbf{drift} and $\Delta$ the \textbf{diffusion coefficient}. Its quadratic variation is $[X,X](t) = \int_0^t\Delta(u)^2\,du$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[It\^o's lemma]
Let $f(t,x)\in C^{1,2}$, i.e.\ with continuous $f_t, f_x, f_{xx}$, and let $X$ be an It\^o process. Then
\begin{align}
    df(t, X(t)) = f_t\, dt + f_x\, dX + \tfrac{1}{2}f_{xx}\, (dX)^2,
\end{align}
where products are evaluated by the multiplication table
\begin{align}
    dt\cdot dt = 0, \qquad dW\cdot dt = 0, \qquad dW\cdot dW = dt,
\end{align}
so that $(dX)^2 = \Delta^2\,dt$. Equivalently, in integrated form,
\begin{align}
    f(t,X(t)) = f(0,X(0)) + \int_0^t\!\left[f_t + \Theta f_x + \tfrac12\Delta^2 f_{xx}\right] du + \int_0^t \Delta f_x\, dW(u).
\end{align}
For a product of two It\^o processes,
\begin{align}
    d(XY) = X\, dY + Y\, dX + dX\cdot dY,
\end{align}
and in $d$ dimensions, with $dW_i\,dW_j = \rho_{ij}\,dt$,
\begin{align}
    df(t,\vec{X}(t)) = f_t\,dt + \sum_i f_{x_i}\,dX_i + \tfrac12\sum_{i,j} f_{x_ix_j}\,dX_i\,dX_j.
\end{align}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
Expand $f$ to second order along a partition:
\begin{align}
    f(t_{j+1},X_{j+1}) - f(t_j,X_j) \approx f_t\,\Delta t_j + f_x\,\Delta X_j + \tfrac12 f_{xx}(\Delta X_j)^2 + \tfrac12 f_{tt}(\Delta t_j)^2 + f_{tx}\Delta t_j\Delta X_j + \cdots
\end{align}
Summing, the terms in $(\Delta t_j)^2$ and $\Delta t_j \Delta X_j$ vanish in the limit. The novel feature is $\sum_j (\Delta X_j)^2$, which does \emph{not} vanish: it converges in probability to the quadratic variation $\int_0^t \Delta^2\,du$, because Brownian motion accumulates squared increments at rate $dt$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Geometric Brownian motion]
Suppose
\begin{align}
    dS(t) = \mu S(t)\, dt + \sigma S(t)\, dW(t),
\end{align}
with $\mu,\sigma$ constant. We cannot naively apply the chain rule here, so we must make a clever guess to get $S(t)$ on one side. Take $f(S) = \log S$, so $f' = 1/S$ and $f'' = -1/S^2$. By It\^o's lemma,
\begin{align}
    d(\log S) &= \frac{1}{S}\, dS - \frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{S^2}(dS)^2 \\
    &= \frac{1}{S}\bigl(\mu S\, dt + \sigma S\, dW\bigr) - \frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{S^2}\sigma^2 S^2\, dt \\
    &= \Bigl(\mu - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\Bigr) dt + \sigma\, dW(t).
\end{align}
The right-hand side has constant coefficients and integrates directly, giving \textbf{geometric Brownian motion}
\begin{align}
    S(t) = S(0)\exp\Bigl(\bigl(\mu - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\bigr)t + \sigma W(t)\Bigr).
\end{align}
Since $\log S(t)$ is Gaussian, $S(t)$ is lognormal. Note that $\mathbb{E}[S(t)] = S(0)e^{\mu t}$: the $-\tfrac12\sigma^2 t$ in the exponent is exactly cancelled by $\mathbb{E}[e^{\sigma W(t)}] = e^{\sigma^2 t/2}$, so the arithmetic mean grows at $\mu$.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Volatility Drag]\label{rmk:vol-drag}
The It\^o correction impacts the three different ``growth rates'' for the same process:
\begin{align}
    \underbrace{\frac{1}{t}\log\mathbb{E}[S(t)/S(0)] = \mu}_{\text{arithmetic (mean)}},
    \qquad
    \underbrace{\frac{1}{t}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\log\frac{S(t)}{S(0)}\right] = \mu - \tfrac12\sigma^2}_{\text{geometric (typical path)}},
\end{align}
and the median of $S(t)$ is $S(0)e^{(\mu-\sigma^2/2)t}$. The gap $\tfrac12\sigma^2$ is \textbf{volatility drag}, arising from the concavity of the logarithm. Reporting the arithmetic mean of a volatile strategy's returns systematically overstates what a compounding investor actually experiences, and the overstatement grows with the square of volatility.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Kelly Criterion]
Let a portfolio hold a fraction $L$ (the leverage) in a risky asset with dynamics $dS/S = \mu\,dt+\sigma\,dW$ and the remainder at the risk-free rate $r$. Then $dV/V = [r + L(\mu-r)]\,dt + L\sigma\,dW$, and by Remark~\ref{rmk:vol-drag} the long-run growth rate of wealth is
\begin{align}
    g(L) = r + L(\mu - r) - \tfrac12 L^2\sigma^2,
\end{align}
which is maximized at the optimal leverage or \textbf{Kelly criterion}
\begin{align}
    L^\star = \frac{\mu-r}{\sigma^2}, \qquad g(L^\star) = r + \frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\mu-r}{\sigma}\right)^{\!2} = r + \tfrac12 \mathrm{SR}^2 .
\end{align}
\end{corollary}

\begin{theorem}[Girsanov]
Let $W$ be Brownian motion on $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})$ with filtration $\mathcal{F}(t)$ over $[0,T]$, let $\Theta$ be adapted, and define
\begin{align}
    Z(t) &= \exp\Bigl(-\int_0^t \Theta(u)\, dW(u) - \tfrac{1}{2}\int_0^t \Theta(u)^2\, du\Bigr), \\
    \widetilde{W}(t) &= W(t) + \int_0^t \Theta(u)\, du.
\end{align}
If $Z$ is a true martingale --- for which \textbf{Novikov's condition}
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}\!\left[\exp\Bigl(\tfrac12\int_0^T\Theta(u)^2\,du\Bigr)\right] < \infty
\end{align}
is sufficient --- then $\mathbb{E}[Z(T)] = 1$, and $\widetilde{W}$ is a Brownian motion under the equivalent measure $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$ defined by $d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}/d\mathbb{P} = Z(T)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof idea]
By It\^o, $dZ = -\Theta Z\,dW$, so $Z$ is a positive local martingale with $Z(0)=1$; Novikov's condition upgrades it to a true martingale, which is what makes $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$ a probability measure. One then checks that $\widetilde{W}$ is a $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$-martingale with continuous paths and $[\widetilde{W},\widetilde{W}](t)=t$, and applies L\'evy's characterization of Brownian motion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Girsanov Changes Drift, Never Volatility]\label{rmk:girsanov-vol}
The most important structural fact about Girsanov is what it leaves alone. The transformation adds a finite-variation term $\int\Theta\,du$ to $W$, and finite-variation terms contribute nothing to quadratic variation, so
\begin{align}
    [\widetilde{W},\widetilde{W}](t) = [W,W](t) = t .
\end{align}
Since quadratic variation is a pathwise limit and equivalent measures agree on which events are null, \emph{volatility is invariant under change of measure} while drift is not.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Martingale representation]
Let $\{\mathcal{F}(t)\}$ be the filtration generated by the Brownian motion $W$, and let $M(t)$ be a square-integrable $\mathcal{F}(t)$-martingale. Then there exists an adapted process $\Gamma$ with $\mathbb{E}\!\left[\int_0^T\Gamma^2\,du\right]<\infty$ such that
\begin{align}
    M(t) = M(0) + \int_0^t \Gamma(u)\, dW(u), \qquad 0\leq t\leq T.
\end{align}
The same holds under $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$ with $\widetilde{W}$ in place of $W$.
\end{theorem}


\subsection{The SDE--PDE Connection}

The deepest structural result of the chapter links stochastic differential equations to partial differential equations. Practically, it means a price may be computed either by simulating many paths and averaging (Monte Carlo) or by solving one deterministic PDE on a grid (finite differences) --- the two being different numerical routes to the same object.

\begin{proposition}[Existence, uniqueness, and the Markov property]
Consider
\begin{align}
    dX(u) = \beta(u, X(u))\, du + \gamma(u, X(u))\, dW(u), \qquad X(t) = x.
\end{align}
If $\beta,\gamma$ are Lipschitz in $x$ uniformly in $u$ and satisfy the linear growth bound $|\beta(u,x)| + |\gamma(u,x)| \leq C(1+|x|)$, then a unique strong solution exists on $[t,T]$, and $X$ is a Markov process.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
Lipschitz continuity gives uniqueness --- it fails for $dX = |X|^{\alpha}dW$ with $\alpha<1/2$,. Linear growth prevents explosion in finite time; without it solutions can reach infinity, which is the same superlinear-growth pathology that produced strict local martingales in Remark~\ref{rmk:local-mart}.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Feynman--Kac]
Let $X$ solve the SDE above and let $h$ be Borel-measurable with suitable growth. Define
\begin{align}
    g(t,x) = \mathbb{E}\!\left[h(X(T)) \mid X(t) = x\right].
\end{align}
Then $g$ satisfies the terminal-value problem
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial g}{\partial t} + \beta(t,x)\frac{\partial g}{\partial x} + \frac{1}{2}\gamma(t,x)^2 \frac{\partial^2 g}{\partial x^2} = 0, \qquad g(T,x) = h(x).
\end{align}
With discounting at rate $r$, $f(t,x) = \mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{-r(T-t)}h(X(T)) \mid X(t)=x\right]$ satisfies
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial f}{\partial t} + \beta(t,x)\frac{\partial f}{\partial x} + \frac{1}{2}\gamma(t,x)^2 \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x^2} = rf, \qquad f(T,x) = h(x).
\end{align}
Conversely, a solution of the PDE with sufficient regularity and growth admits the stochastic representation above.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof idea]
Apply It\^o's lemma to $e^{-rt}f(t,X(t))$:
\begin{align}
    d\!\left[e^{-rt}f\right] = e^{-rt}\!\left[f_t + \beta f_x + \tfrac12\gamma^2 f_{xx} - rf\right]dt + e^{-rt}\gamma f_x\,dW .
\end{align}
By the tower property, $e^{-rt}f(t,X(t))$ is a martingale, and a martingale of finite variation is constant, so the $dt$ coefficient must vanish identically --- which is the PDE. The converse runs the argument backwards: assume the PDE, conclude the process is a local martingale, and use the growth condition to upgrade to a true martingale so that $f(t,x)=\mathbb{E}[\cdot]$ holds.
\end{proof}

Feynman--Kac is a special case of a general correspondence between Markov processes and differential operators, which provides a single language for pricing across models.

\begin{definition}[Semigroup]
For a time-homogeneous Markov process $X$, define the \textbf{semigroup operators}
\begin{align}
    (P_t f)(x) = \mathbb{E}[f(X(t)) \mid X(0) = x].
\end{align}
These satisfy $P_0 = \mathrm{id}$ and the \textbf{Chapman--Kolmogorov} property $P_{s+t} = P_s P_t$. For time-inhomogeneous processes there is a two-parameter family with $P_{t,T} = P_{t,s}P_{s,T}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proof}[Proof of the semigroup property]
$P_0f(x) = \mathbb{E}[f(X(0))\mid X(0)=x] = f(x)$. For the composition, by the tower property and then the Markov property,
\begin{align}
    P_{s+t}f(x) = \mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbb{E}[f(X(s+t))\mid \mathcal{F}(s)]\bigm| X(0)=x\bigr] = \mathbb{E}\bigl[(P_tf)(X(s))\bigm| X(0)=x\bigr] = P_s(P_tf)(x).
\end{align}
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Infinitesimal generator]
The \textbf{generator} of the semigroup is
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{L}f(x) = \lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{P_tf(x)-f(x)}{t} = \lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}[f(X(t))\mid X(0)=x]-f(x)}{t},
\end{align}
defined on the set of $f$ for which the limit exists in the appropriate norm. The \textbf{adjoint} $\mathcal{L}^*$ acts on densities and is defined by $\langle\mathcal{L}f,p\rangle = \langle f,\mathcal{L}^*p\rangle$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Generator of a diffusion]
For $dX = \beta(t,x)\,dt+\gamma(t,x)\,dW$,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{L}_t f(x) = \beta(t,x)f'(x) + \tfrac12\gamma(t,x)^2f''(x),
\end{align}
with adjoint
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{L}^*_t p(t,x) = -\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\bigl(\beta(t,x)p\bigr) + \frac12\frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2}\bigl(\gamma(t,x)^2 p\bigr).
\end{align}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof idea]
Apply It\^o's lemma to $f(X(u))$ over $[0,t]$, take expectations so the $dW$ integral drops out, divide by $t$ and let $t\downarrow 0$; the $du$ integrand at $u=0$ is $\beta f' + \tfrac12\gamma^2f''$. The adjoint follows by integrating $\int(\mathcal{L}f)p\,dx$ by parts twice and discarding boundary terms.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Dynkin's formula]\label{prop:dynkin}
For $f$ in the domain of the generator and a stopping time $\tau$ with $\mathbb{E}[\tau]<\infty$,
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}_x\!\left[f(X(\tau))\right] = f(x) + \mathbb{E}_x\!\left[\int_0^{\tau}\mathcal{L}_u f(X(u))\,du\right].
\end{align}
In particular, $\dfrac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_x[f(X(t))] = \mathbb{E}_x[\mathcal{L}_tf(X(t))]$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
It\^o's lemma gives $f(X(t)) = f(x) + \int_0^t \mathcal{L}_uf(X(u))\,du + \int_0^t \gamma f'(X(u))\,dW(u)$. The stochastic integral is a martingale under the stated integrability, so it has zero expectation; optional stopping extends this to $\tau$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Affine process]
A Markov process is \textbf{affine} if
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{uX(t)}\mid X(0)=x\right] = e^{A(t,u)+B(t,u)x}
\end{align}
for deterministic $A,B$, equivalently if $\mathcal{L}e^{ux} = (a(u)+b(u)x)e^{ux}$, i.e.\ if the drift and the \emph{squared} diffusion are both affine in the state.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Kolmogorov backward equation]
Let $u(t,x) = \mathbb{E}[f(X(T))\mid X(t)=x]$ for $t\leq T$. Then
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial u}{\partial t} + \mathcal{L}_tu = 0, \qquad u(T,x) = f(x),
\end{align}
and with discounting, $u(t,x)=\mathbb{E}[e^{-r(T-t)}f(X(T))\mid X(t)=x]$ satisfies $\partial_t u + \mathcal{L}_tu - ru = 0$. The backward equation runs in the \emph{backward} variables $(t,x)$ and governs the evolution of \emph{observables} --- prices and value functions.
\end{theorem}

% [CORRECTED] The previous proof of the forward equation was not valid: it
% cited the backward equation (the wrong starting point -- one needs Dynkin's
% formula), and it wrote the derivative and density in the backward variables
% (t,x) when the forward equation is an equation in the FORWARD variables
% (T,y), with the initial point (t,x) held fixed. Both are repaired below.
\begin{theorem}[Kolmogorov forward equation / Fokker--Planck]
Fix the initial point $(t,x)$. The transition density $p(t,T,x,y)$, viewed as a function of the \emph{forward} variables $(T,y)$, satisfies
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial p}{\partial T} = \mathcal{L}^*_T\,p,
\end{align}
explicitly
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial p}{\partial T} = -\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\bigl(\beta(T,y)\,p\bigr) + \frac12\frac{\partial^2}{\partial y^2}\bigl(\gamma(T,y)^2\,p\bigr),
\end{align}
with initial condition $\lim_{T\downarrow t}p(t,T,x,y) = \delta(y-x)$. The forward equation governs the evolution of \emph{probability distributions}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $(t,x)$ and let $f$ be an arbitrary smooth compactly supported test function. By Dynkin's formula (Proposition~\ref{prop:dynkin}), differentiating in the forward time variable $T$,
\begin{align}
    \frac{d}{dT}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[f(X(T))\mid X(t)=x\right] = \mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathcal{L}_Tf(X(T))\mid X(t)=x\right].
\end{align}
Writing both sides as integrals against the transition density in the forward variable $y$,
\begin{align}
    \int f(y)\,\frac{\partial p(t,T,x,y)}{\partial T}\,dy = \int \bigl(\mathcal{L}_Tf\bigr)(y)\,p(t,T,x,y)\,dy = \int f(y)\,\bigl(\mathcal{L}^*_T\,p\bigr)(t,T,x,y)\,dy,
\end{align}
the last step being the definition of the adjoint. Since this holds for every test function $f$, the integrands agree, giving $\partial_T p = \mathcal{L}^*_Tp$. The initial condition is the statement that $X(t)=x$ with certainty.
\end{proof}

\section{Jump Diffusion}

Pure diffusion models imply that prices move continuously: there are no sudden jumps, gaps, or discontinuities. This is mathematically convenient but empirically inadequate. Markets experience flash crashes, earnings surprises, central bank announcements, and credit events that cause instantaneous price jumps. Modifying the price process to include jumps captures these features while retaining much of the diffusion framework.

\subsection{L\'evy Processes and Poisson Jumps}

\begin{definition}
A function $f$ is \textbf{c\`adl\`ag} (\emph{continue \`a droite, limite \`a gauche}) if it is right-continuous with left limits at every point. C\`adl\`ag paths allow for jumps: the function value equals the right limit, and the left limit captures the ``pre-jump'' value.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
A stochastic process $X(t)$ on a filtered probability space is a \textbf{L\'evy process} if:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $X(0) = 0$ a.s.,
    \item paths are c\`adl\`ag a.s.,
    \item $X(t)$ is adapted,
    \item increments $X(t) - X(s)$ are independent of $\mathcal{F}(s)$,
    \item increments are stationary: $X(t) - X(s) \overset{d}{=} X(t-s)$,
    \item stochastic continuity: $\lim_{h \to 0} \mathbb{P}(|X(t+h) - X(t)| > \epsilon) = 0$ for all $\epsilon > 0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
L\'evy processes generalize both Brownian motion (continuous, Gaussian increments) and Poisson processes (discontinuous, integer-valued increments).
\end{remark}

% [ADDED] Stochastic continuity is a standard trap: it does NOT mean continuous
% paths. Worth stating explicitly, and it has a real financial consequence.
\begin{remark}[Stochastic Continuity Does Not Mean Continuous Paths]
Property (6) is easily misread. It does \emph{not} say the paths are continuous --- the Poisson process satisfies it and jumps. What it rules out is jumps at \emph{deterministic} times: for each \emph{fixed} $t$, $\mathbb{P}(\Delta X(t) \neq 0) = 0$. Jumps occur, but their arrival times are random, and no particular clock time is singled out in advance.

This has a direct modelling consequence. A plain L\'evy process is therefore the \emph{wrong} model for a scheduled announcement --- an earnings release at 4:00pm, an FOMC decision at 2:00pm --- where a jump is certain to occur at a known time, with only its size random. Such events require a time-inhomogeneous jump process with a deterministic jump time, or a separate event-risk overlay. The stationary-increments assumption (5) is violated for the same reason: the announcement window is not statistically like any other window of equal length.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}
There exists a stochastic process $N(t)$, called the \textbf{Poisson process} with intensity $\lambda > 0$, such that $N(0) = 0$ a.s., $N(t)$ has c\`adl\`ag paths, independent increments, and $N(t) - N(s) \sim \text{Pois}(\lambda(t-s))$.
\end{theorem}

% [EXPANDED] The original said "and conversely" without saying what the
% converse is. It is thinning, and it is worth stating properly.
\begin{proposition}[Superposition and Thinning]
\emph{(Superposition.)} If $N_1(t)$ and $N_2(t)$ are independent Poisson processes with intensities $\lambda_1, \lambda_2$, then $N_1(t) + N_2(t)$ is a Poisson process with intensity $\lambda_1 + \lambda_2$. Moreover, two independent Poisson processes almost surely never jump at the same time.

\emph{(Thinning, the converse.)} If $N(t)$ is a Poisson process with intensity $\lambda$ and each arrival is independently marked ``type 1'' with probability $p$ and ``type 2'' with probability $1-p$, then the resulting counting processes $N_1(t)$ and $N_2(t)$ are \emph{independent} Poisson processes with intensities $p\lambda$ and $(1-p)\lambda$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
The independence in the thinning result is the surprising part: knowing how many type-1 arrivals occurred tells you nothing about the number of type-2 arrivals, despite both being carved out of the same stream. Operationally, superposition and thinning are what let one aggregate or disaggregate jump risk by source --- e.g.\ decomposing a firm's total jump intensity into idiosyncratic and market-wide components, or pooling independent default intensities across a portfolio.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
Let $N(t)$ be a Poisson process with intensity $\lambda$, and let $(Y_k)_{k \geq 1}$ be i.i.d.\ random variables with mean $\beta$, independent of $N(t)$. The process
\begin{align}
    Q(t) = \sum_{k=1}^{N(t)} Y_k
\end{align}
is a \textbf{compound Poisson process}. It counts random arrivals and assigns each one a random size.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Think of $N(t)$ as counting ``shocks'' (earnings announcements, credit events, policy changes) and $Y_k$ as the size of each shock. The compound Poisson process models both the timing and magnitude of jumps.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{compensated Poisson process} $M(t) = N(t) - \lambda t$ is a martingale: its expected increment is zero. For a compound Poisson process, the compensated martingale is $Q(t) - \lambda \beta t$.
\end{definition}

% [CORRECTED] The original said compensation "parallels the role of Girsanov's
% theorem." It does not: compensation is a Doob-Meyer decomposition WITHIN a
% fixed measure; Girsanov CHANGES the measure. Conflating them obscures the
% key fact that the jump analogue of Girsanov is genuinely richer (it tilts the
% intensity AND the jump-size law), which is the source of incompleteness.
\begin{remark}[Compensation is Doob--Meyer, Not Girsanov]
Compensation subtracts the predictable compensator $\lambda t$, leaving a zero-mean martingale. This is the \textbf{Doob--Meyer decomposition} of the submartingale $N(t)$ into a predictable finite-variation part and a martingale part, and it takes place \emph{within a fixed measure} $\mathbb{P}$. The correct diffusion analogue is the observation that $\int_0^t \sigma(s)\,dW(s)$ is the martingale part of an It\^o process --- not Girsanov's theorem.

Girsanov is a different operation: it \emph{changes the measure}. For a diffusion, the change of measure has exactly one degree of freedom --- it shifts the drift of $W$, leaving the volatility invariant. For a jump process it has far more. Under an equivalent change of measure one may:
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep]
    \item rescale the intensity, $\lambda^{\mathbb{P}} \to \lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}$; and
    \item tilt the jump-size distribution, $\nu^{\mathbb{P}}(dx) \to \nu^{\mathbb{Q}}(dx)$, subject only to $\nu^{\mathbb{Q}} \sim \nu^{\mathbb{P}}$.
\end{enumerate}
The Radon--Nikodym density for a compound Poisson process takes the form
\begin{align}
    \frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\bigg|_{\mathcal{F}(t)} = \exp\left\{-(\lambda^{\mathbb{Q}} - \lambda^{\mathbb{P}})t\right\}\prod_{k=1}^{N(t)} \frac{\lambda^{\mathbb{Q}} f^{\mathbb{Q}}(Y_k)}{\lambda^{\mathbb{P}} f^{\mathbb{P}}(Y_k)},
\end{align}
where $f^{\mathbb{P}}, f^{\mathbb{Q}}$ are the jump-size densities. This infinite-dimensional freedom is precisely why jump-diffusion markets are \emph{incomplete}: there are infinitely many equivalent martingale measures, and the no-arbitrage condition alone does not select one. See Section~\ref{sec:incomplete-markets}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Jump Diffusions and Extended It\^o Calculus}

% [CORRECTED] The original introduced nu but never linked it to N -- nu appeared
% nowhere in the formula. The link is that N is a Poisson random measure with
% INTENSITY nu(dx)dt. Also added the characteristic triplet and Levy-Khintchine,
% which is the whole point of the decomposition.
\begin{theorem}[L\'evy--It\^o Decomposition]
Let $N(dt, dx)$ be the jump measure of $X$, i.e.\ the random counting measure on $[0,\infty) \times \mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$ that places a unit mass at $(s, \Delta X(s))$ for each jump time $s$. Then $N$ is a \textbf{Poisson random measure} with \textbf{intensity measure} $\nu(dx)\,dt$, where $\nu$ is the \textbf{L\'evy measure}, satisfying $\nu(\{0\}) = 0$ and
\begin{align}
    \int_{\mathbb{R}} \min(1, x^2)\, \nu(dx) < \infty.
\end{align}
Writing $\widetilde{N}(dt,dx) = N(dt,dx) - \nu(dx)\,dt$ for the compensated measure, every L\'evy process admits the decomposition
\begin{align}
    X(t) = \underbrace{bt}_{\text{drift}} + \underbrace{\sigma W(t)}_{\text{diffusion}} + \underbrace{\int_0^t\!\!\int_{|x| < 1} x\, \widetilde{N}(ds, dx)}_{\text{compensated small jumps}} + \underbrace{\int_0^t\!\!\int_{|x| \geq 1} x\, N(ds, dx)}_{\text{large jumps}},
\end{align}
where $b \in \mathbb{R}$, $\sigma \geq 0$, and $W$ is a Brownian motion independent of $N$. The four terms are mutually independent. The triple $(b, \sigma^2, \nu)$ is called the \textbf{characteristic triplet} and determines the law of $X$ uniquely.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[L\'evy--Khintchine Formula]
The characteristic function of a L\'evy process with triplet $(b,\sigma^2,\nu)$ is
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{iuX(t)}\right] = \exp\left\{ t\left( ibu - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2 u^2 + \int_{\mathbb{R}} \left( e^{iux} - 1 - iux\,\mathbf{1}_{\{|x|<1\}} \right) \nu(dx) \right) \right\}.
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[Why This Is the Workhorse Result]
L\'evy--Khintchine is the reason exponential-L\'evy models are tractable in practice. The characteristic function is available in closed form even when the density is not, which is exactly the input required by Fourier-transform pricing methods (Carr--Madan, COS). One prices options under VG, CGMY, or NIG dynamics not by knowing the density but by knowing $\hat{\phi}$ and inverting numerically.
\end{remark}

% [CORRECTED] The original said small jumps "have finite quadratic variation, SO
% they are compensated to ensure convergence." That inverts the logic: finite QV
% is why compensation WORKS, not why it is NEEDED. It is needed because the small
% jumps may have infinite FIRST-order variation.
\begin{remark}[Why Small Jumps Must Be Compensated]
The L\'evy measure characterizes the jump behavior: $\nu(A)$ is the expected number of jumps per unit time with sizes in $A$. The two integrals are treated asymmetrically for a real reason.

Large jumps ($|x| \geq 1$) satisfy $\nu(\{|x| \geq 1\}) < \infty$ by the integrability condition, so they arrive at a finite rate --- finitely many in any bounded interval --- and can simply be summed. No compensation is needed, and indeed compensating them could fail, since $\int_{|x|\geq 1} x\,\nu(dx)$ need not be finite (the process may have infinite mean).

Small jumps ($|x| < 1$) are the delicate case. The integrability condition permits $\int_{|x|<1} |x|\,\nu(dx) = \infty$, i.e.\ \emph{infinite first-order variation}: the raw sum $\sum_{s \leq t, |\Delta X(s)| < 1} \Delta X(s)$ need not converge absolutely. Compensation is therefore a \emph{necessity}, not a convenience --- it recenters each increment to mean zero, turning a divergent sum into an $L^2$-bounded martingale. What makes that $L^2$ convergence go through is precisely $\int_{|x|<1} x^2\,\nu(dx) < \infty$: finite quadratic variation is why compensation \emph{works}, not why it is \emph{required}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Finite versus Infinite Activity]
The dichotomy above corresponds to a modelling choice that matters in practice:
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
    \item \textbf{Finite activity}: $\nu(\mathbb{R}) < \infty$. Jumps arrive at a finite rate $\lambda = \nu(\mathbb{R})$ and the process is a compound Poisson process plus a diffusion. Merton (1976) and Kou (2002) are of this type. Compensation is a convenience.
    \item \textbf{Infinite activity}: $\nu(\mathbb{R}) = \infty$. Infinitely many (mostly tiny) jumps occur in any interval; there is no ``first'' jump and no well-defined arrival rate. Variance Gamma, CGMY, and NIG are of this type. Compensation is unavoidable. Such models can fit the returns distribution with $\sigma = 0$ --- no Brownian component at all --- since the small jumps themselves generate the diffusive behaviour.
\end{itemize}
Empirically the question of whether asset prices have a diffusion component at all, or are pure-jump infinite-activity processes, is genuinely contested; it is not settled by looking at a price chart.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{jump diffusion} combines continuous Brownian fluctuations with discrete Poisson jumps:
\begin{align}
    X(t) = X(0) + \int_0^t \mu(s)\, ds + \int_0^t \sigma(s)\, dW(s) + \sum_{k=1}^{N(t)} Y_k,
\end{align}
where $W(t)$, $N(t)$, and the $Y_k$ are mutually independent. Write
\begin{align}
    X^c(t) := X(0) + \int_0^t \mu(s)\,ds + \int_0^t \sigma(s)\,dW(s)
\end{align}
for the \textbf{continuous part}, so that $X(t) = X^c(t) + \sum_{k=1}^{N(t)} Y_k$, and note that the continuous quadratic variation is $d[X]^c_t = \sigma^2(t)\,dt$. This is a finite-activity jump diffusion: the jump term is a compound Poisson process.
\end{definition}

% [CORRECTED -- THIS WAS THE SERIOUS ONE] The original integrated against the
% CONTINUOUS part dX^c AND subtracted f'(X_{s-}) Delta X(s) in the jump sum.
% Those are two incompatible conventions spliced together: the subtraction
% removes a first-order jump contribution that dX^c never supplied, so the
% jumps cancel out entirely. Sanity check with f(x) = x: the original gives
% X(t) - X(0) = X^c(t), losing every jump. Both correct forms are given below.
\begin{theorem}[It\^o's Lemma with Jumps]
Let $X(t)$ be a jump diffusion and $f \in C^2(\mathbb{R})$. Then, in integrated form, the following two equivalent statements hold.

\emph{Form A (integrating against the continuous part).} Since $dX^c$ carries no jumps, the jump sum must supply the \emph{entire} jump contribution:
\begin{align}
    f(X(t)) - f(X(0)) = \int_0^t f'(X(s^-))\, dX^c(s) + \frac{1}{2}\int_0^t f''(X(s^-))\, \sigma^2(s)\, ds + \sum_{0 < s \leq t} \Bigl[f(X(s)) - f(X(s^-))\Bigr].
\end{align}

\emph{Form B (integrating against the full process).} Since $\int_0^t f'(X(s^-))\,dX(s)$ already contains $\sum_{0<s\leq t} f'(X(s^-))\Delta X(s)$, that first-order term must be \emph{subtracted back out} of the jump sum to avoid double-counting:
\begin{align}
    f(X(t)) - f(X(0)) = \int_0^t f'(X(s^-))\, dX(s) + \frac{1}{2}\int_0^t f''(X(s^-))\, \sigma^2(s)\, ds + \sum_{0 < s \leq t} \Bigl[f(X(s)) - f(X(s^-)) - f'(X(s^-))\Delta X(s)\Bigr],
\end{align}
where $\Delta X(s) = X(s) - X(s^-)$ is the jump size.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[The Two Forms Must Not Be Mixed]
The distinction is a standard source of error: one either integrates against $dX^c$ and adds the \emph{full} jump $f(X(s)) - f(X(s^-))$, or integrates against $dX$ and adds the \emph{compensated} jump $f(X(s)) - f(X(s^-)) - f'(X(s^-))\Delta X(s)$. Using $dX^c$ \emph{and} subtracting $f'(X(s^-))\Delta X(s)$ deletes the jumps altogether.

A one-line sanity check catches this every time: set $f(x) = x$, so $f' \equiv 1$ and $f'' \equiv 0$. Form A returns $X(t) - X(0) = X^c(t) - X^c(0) + \sum \Delta X(s)$, which is the definition of the process. Form B returns $X(t)-X(0) = \int_0^t dX(s) + 0$, likewise correct. Any version that returns only the continuous part is wrong.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Interpretation of the Jump Term]
The jump term \emph{supplements} the second-order Taylor term rather than replacing it: the It\^o correction $\tfrac12 f'' \sigma^2\,dt$ is generated by the continuous martingale part and survives untouched. What the jump sum adds is the exact, nonlinear change in $f$ across each discontinuity, $f(X(s)) - f(X(s^-))$, evaluated at the realized jump size --- \emph{not} a Taylor approximation to it. This is exactly why jump-diffusion option prices involve an expectation over the jump-size \emph{distribution} rather than a Gaussian integral: the payoff must be re-evaluated at each possible post-jump price, not linearized around the pre-jump one. It is also why $f$ need only be $C^2$ --- the jump term requires no smoothness at all, merely evaluation of $f$ at two points.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Compensated Form and the Integro-Differential Generator]
Taking expectations of Form B and using that the compensated jump measure integrates to zero yields the infinitesimal generator of a jump diffusion,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{A}f(x) = \mu(x) f'(x) + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2(x) f''(x) + \int_{\mathbb{R}} \Bigl[ f(x + y) - f(x) - y f'(x)\mathbf{1}_{\{|y|<1\}} \Bigr] \nu(dy).
\end{align}
The nonlocal integral term is what turns the Feynman--Kac PDE of the pure-diffusion case into a \textbf{partial integro-differential equation} (PIDE). Numerically this is the whole difficulty: the integral term couples every point of the grid to every other, destroying the tridiagonal structure that makes standard finite-difference schemes cheap.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Merton's Jump-Diffusion Model]
In Merton's (1976) model, the stock price follows
\begin{align}
    \frac{dS}{S(t^-)} = (\mu - \lambda \beta)\, dt + \sigma\, dW(t) + J\, dN(t),
\end{align}
where $J = e^Y - 1$ for $Y \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_J, \sigma_J^2)$, and $\beta = \mathbb{E}[J] = e^{\mu_J + \sigma_J^2/2} - 1$.

% [CORRECTED] The original said the -lambda*beta drift adjustment "ensures the
% discounted price remains a martingale." It does not, by itself: it ensures the
% total expected return equals mu. The martingale property additionally requires
% mu = r under Q.
The $-\lambda\beta$ term compensates the expected jump contribution, so that
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{dS}{S(t^-)}\right] = (\mu - \lambda\beta)\,dt + \underbrace{\lambda\beta\,dt}_{\mathbb{E}[J\,dN]} = \mu\,dt.
\end{align}
That is, the compensator ensures the \emph{total expected return is exactly} $\mu$, jumps included --- it does \emph{not} on its own make the discounted price a martingale. The martingale property requires additionally setting $\mu = r$, which is the defining property of the risk-neutral measure $\mathbb{Q}$.

Applying It\^o's lemma with jumps (Form A) to $f(S) = \log S$ gives the closed-form solution, which is what one actually simulates:
\begin{align}
    S(t) = S(0)\,\exp\left[\left(\mu - \lambda\beta - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\right)t + \sigma W(t)\right] \prod_{k=1}^{N(t)} e^{Y_k}.
\end{align}
Between jumps the stock follows GBM; at each Poisson arrival the price is multiplied by $e^{Y_k}$. Conditional on $N(T) = k$, the log-price is Gaussian with variance $\sigma^2 T + k\sigma_J^2$, so the unconditional return distribution is a Poisson mixture of Gaussians --- leptokurtic, and left-skewed when $\mu_J < 0$. This is what generates the heavy tails and the short-dated smile that pure GBM cannot produce.
\end{example}

% [ADDED] The economically load-bearing assumption in Merton was missing
% entirely. Without it the Poisson-weighted formula is not a unique price.
\begin{remark}[The Assumption That Makes Merton's Formula Work]
Jump risk cannot be hedged by trading $S$ and the bond alone: a single stock and a bond span the Brownian shock but not the jump, so the market is \textbf{incomplete} and there is a continuum of equivalent martingale measures, each giving a different arbitrage-free option price. No-arbitrage alone does not pin down a price.

Merton closes the model by \emph{assuming that jump risk is idiosyncratic and therefore diversifiable}, hence carries \emph{zero risk premium}. Under this assumption the jump intensity $\lambda$ and the jump-size law $Y \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_J,\sigma_J^2)$ are \emph{the same under $\mathbb{P}$ and $\mathbb{Q}$}, and only the diffusive drift is adjusted ($\mu \to r$). This is what makes the pricing measure unique and delivers the closed form below. It is an economic assumption, not a mathematical consequence.

It is also empirically false for index options. If jump risk were unpriced, the risk-neutral and physical jump distributions would coincide; instead, the steep index smile implies a risk-neutral jump distribution far more negatively skewed than the physical one --- investors pay a large premium for crash protection. Quantifying that wedge is the jump-risk premium, and relaxing Merton's assumption is the point of departure for Bates (1996) and the stochastic-volatility-with-jumps (SVCJ) literature. See Section~\ref{sec:incomplete-markets} for the general problem of selecting a measure when the market is incomplete.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Merton's European Call Formula]
Under the assumption that jump risk is unpriced, the European call price decomposes as a Poisson-weighted sum of Black--Scholes prices:
\begin{align}
    C_{\text{Merton}} = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-\lambda' T}(\lambda' T)^k}{k!}\, C_{\text{BS}}(S_0, K, T, r_k, \sigma_k),
\end{align}
where, with $\lambda' = \lambda(1+\beta) = \lambda e^{\mu_J + \sigma_J^2/2}$,
\begin{align}
    \sigma_k^2 = \sigma^2 + \frac{k\,\sigma_J^2}{T}, \qquad
    r_k = r - \lambda\beta + \frac{k\ln(1+\beta)}{T} = r - \lambda\beta + \frac{k\left(\mu_J + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_J^2\right)}{T}.
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
The derivation is exactly the conditioning argument the formula suggests: condition on $N(T) = k$; given $k$ jumps, the terminal log-price is Gaussian, so the conditional price is Black--Scholes with the variance inflated by $k\sigma_J^2/T$ and the drift shifted by the realized jump mean; then average over $k$ against the Poisson weights. The intensity appearing in the weights is $\lambda' = \lambda(1+\beta)$, not $\lambda$: the change of measure induced by conditioning tilts the arrival rate by the mean jump multiplier. This is the same size-biasing that appears whenever one conditions on a compound sum.

In practice the series converges geometrically and 10--20 terms suffice for typical parameters ($\lambda \approx 0.1$--$1$ per year). The formula is a genuinely useful benchmark, but note that it can only produce a \emph{smile} that flattens rapidly with maturity, since jump effects wash out under aggregation by the CLT. Fitting the persistent long-dated skew requires stochastic volatility as well --- hence Bates.
\end{remark}


\chapter{Risk-Neutral Formulation}

The mathematical foundations of the previous chapter, like stochastic calculus, Girsanov's theorem, and the martingale representation theorem, are powerful but abstract. This chapter puts them to work.

The answer rests on a single economic principle: no arbitrage. If two portfolios produce the same future payoff in every state of the world, they must have the same price today. This simple statement, combined with the stochastic calculus machinery, yields a complete pricing framework. The key insight is that no-arbitrage is equivalent to the existence of a probability measure (the risk-neutral measure) under which discounted asset prices are martingales. Under this measure, every derivative price is simply a discounted expected payoff.

The chapter proceeds in two stages. First, we develop risk-neutral pricing in the single-asset and multi-asset settings, prove the fundamental theorems of asset pricing, and establish the conditions under which hedging is possible. Second, we generalize the framework via change of num\'eraire, showing that the money market account is just one of many possible reference assets, and that choosing the right num\'eraire can dramatically simplify specific pricing problems.


\section{Risk-Neutral Pricing}

\subsection{One Asset}

We begin with the simplest setting: one stock, one Brownian motion, one risk-free rate.

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{portfolio} (or \textbf{wealth process}) $X(t)$ represents the value of a self-financing combination of a stock $S(t)$ and a money market account earning the risk-free rate $R(t)$. Let $\Delta(t)$ be the number of shares of stock held at time $t$, adapted to the filtration. The remaining wealth $X(t) - \Delta(t)S(t)$ is invested in the money market. The self-financing condition gives the portfolio differential:
\begin{align}
    dX(t) = \Delta(t)\,dS(t) + R(t)\bigl(X(t) - \Delta(t)S(t)\bigr)\,dt.
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
The self-financing condition means that changes in portfolio value come \emph{only} from capital gains on the stock and interest on the money market position; no external cash is added or withdrawn. This is the mathematical embodiment of ``no free lunch,'' that any trading strategy must fund itself.
\end{remark}

To compare values at different times, we need to account for the time value of money.

\begin{definition}
For an adapted interest rate process $R(t)$, the \textbf{discount process} is
\begin{align}
    D(t) = \exp\!\Bigl(-\int_0^t R(u)\,du\Bigr),
\end{align}
with differential $dD(t) = -R(t)D(t)\,dt$. The \textbf{discounted stock price} is $D(t)S(t)$, which removes the effect of the risk-free rate and isolates the ``excess'' dynamics of the stock.
\end{definition}

When the stock follows geometric Brownian motion with drift $\mu(t)$ and volatility $\sigma(t)$, the discounted stock price has an explicit form.

\begin{proposition}
The discounted stock price satisfies
\begin{align}
    D(t)S(t) = S(0)\exp\!\Bigl(\int_0^t\bigl(\mu(s) - R(s) - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma(s)^2\bigr)\,ds + \int_0^t \sigma(s)\,dW(s)\Bigr),
\end{align}
with differential
\begin{align}
    d\bigl(D(t)S(t)\bigr) = \sigma(t)D(t)S(t)\bigl[\Theta(t)\,dt + dW(t)\bigr],
\end{align}
where we write the \textbf{market price of risk} as
\begin{align}
    \Theta(t) = \frac{\mu(t) - R(t)}{\sigma(t)}.
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
The market price of risk $\Theta(t)$ measures the excess return per unit of volatility (the Sharpe ratio of the stock). It quantifies how much compensation the market demands for bearing one unit of diffusive risk. Under the real-world measure, the discounted stock price has a non-zero drift proportional to $\Theta$: it is not a martingale. The entire point of what follows is to remove this drift.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
Apply Girsanov's theorem with $\Theta(t) = (\mu(t)-R(t))/\sigma(t)$ to define
\begin{align}
    \widetilde{W}(t) = W(t) + \int_0^t \Theta(u)\,du.
\end{align}
Under the new measure $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$ (with Radon-Nikodym derivative $Z(T) = \exp(-\int_0^T\Theta\,dW - \frac{1}{2}\int_0^T\Theta^2\,du)$), the process $\widetilde{W}(t)$ is a Brownian motion, and the discounted stock price becomes
\begin{align}
    d\bigl(D(t)S(t)\bigr) = \sigma(t)D(t)S(t)\,d\widetilde{W}(t).
\end{align}
The measure $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$ is called the \textbf{risk-neutral measure} (or \textbf{equivalent martingale measure}).
\end{definition}

\begin{remark} Under $\mathbb{P}$, the stock earns $\mu$; under $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$, it earns $R$. The excess return has been absorbed into the measure change. However, under both measures, the diffusion coefficient is $\sigma(t)S(t)$. This is why hedging depends on volatility (which is observable) rather than drift (which is not). The discounted stock price $D(t)S(t)$ is now a martingale under $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$, as its $dt$ coefficient is zero.
\end{remark}

The martingale property extends from the stock to any self-financing portfolio.

\begin{proposition}
Discounted portfolio values are martingales under the risk-neutral measure:
\begin{align}
    d\bigl(D(t)X(t)\bigr) = \Delta(t)\sigma(t)D(t)S(t)\,d\widetilde{W}(t).
\end{align}
In particular, every self-financing portfolio earns the risk-free rate on average under $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
This is the mathematical formalization of ``risk-neutral'': under $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}$, investors are indifferent to risk, demanding no premium beyond the risk-free rate.
