    \mu_i(t) = -\sigma_i(t)\,L_i(t)\sum_{j=i+1}^{N-1}\frac{\delta_j L_j(t)\,\sigma_j(t)\,\rho_{ij}}{1 + \delta_j L_j(t)}.
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
The drift of $L_i$ depends on all subsequent rates $L_{i+1}, \ldots, L_{N-1}$. This coupling is the principal analytical difficulty of the LMM: while each rate is individually lognormal under its own measure, the joint system is not jointly lognormal under any single measure. As a result, multi-rate products (swaptions, Bermudans) generally require Monte Carlo simulation rather than closed-form solutions.
\end{remark}

The LMM prices single-rate products in closed form.

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{caplet} on the period $[T_i, T_{i+1}]$ with strike $K$ pays $\delta_i\max(L_i(T_i) - K, 0)$ at time $T_{i+1}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
Under $\mathbb{Q}^{(T_{i+1})}$, the caplet prices as
\begin{align}
    V_{\text{cap}}(t) = B(t,T_{i+1})\,\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}^{(T_{i+1})}\bigl[\delta_i\max(L_i(T_i)-K,0)\;\big|\;\mathcal{F}(t)\bigr].
\end{align}
Since $L_i$ is lognormal under $\mathbb{Q}^{(T_{i+1})}$, the price is given by the \textbf{Black formula} (the same formula as Black-Scholes but with the forward rate replacing the stock price and the bond price replacing the discount factor).
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
The LMM prices caplets consistently with the Black formula that traders already use, while providing a self-consistent joint dynamics for all rates. Swaption pricing is more complex because the swap rate is a nonlinear function of multiple LIBOR rates.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{forward swap rate} for a swap starting at $T_\alpha$ and ending at $T_\beta$ is
\begin{align}
    S_{\alpha,\beta}(t) = \frac{B(t,T_\alpha) - B(t,T_\beta)}{\sum_{k=\alpha}^{\beta-1}\delta_k\,B(t,T_{k+1})}.
\end{align}
No measure exists under which $S_{\alpha,\beta}$ is exactly lognormal (it is a ratio of sums of lognormals).
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Calibration procedure]
LMM calibration proceeds in three steps: (1) calibrate individual volatilities $\sigma_i(t)$ to caplet implied volatilities, (2) fit the correlation matrix $(\rho_{ij})$ to swaption prices (often using a low-rank parameterization for tractability), and (3) validate the joint dynamics by Monte Carlo pricing of exotic payoffs (Bermudans, callable range accruals, CMS products).
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Post-LIBOR transition]
Although LIBOR has been discontinued and replaced by backward-looking rates such as SOFR, the modeling framework survives in adapted forms. SOFR-based forwards are compounded in arrears rather than set in advance, requiring modifications to the LMM dynamics and payoff timing.
\end{remark}


\section{Credit Risk Modeling}

The fixed-income framework thus far has treated bond payments as certain. In reality an issuer may default, and the bondholder then receives only a fraction of the promised cash flows. This section builds models for the random default time $\tau$ and applies them to credit-sensitive instruments.

Two paradigms dominate:

\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
    \item \textbf{Structural models} treat default as \emph{endogenous}: it is triggered by an observable process --- the firm's asset value --- crossing a threshold. Default is therefore predictable from the state variable, which ties credit to equity markets and gives the model economic content.
    \item \textbf{Reduced-form models} treat default as \emph{exogenous}: an unpredictable arrival governed by a hazard rate, with no attempt to explain \emph{why} it occurs. This sacrifices economic interpretation for tractability and calibration fit.
\end{itemize}

As Remark~\ref{rmk:accessible} below makes precise, this is at bottom a distinction between \emph{predictable} and \emph{totally inaccessible} stopping times, and it fully explains why the two classes behave so differently at short maturities.

\subsection{Structural Models}

\begin{theorem}[Merton Model]
Let the firm's asset value follow geometric Brownian motion,
\begin{align}
    dA(t) = \mu A(t)\,dt + \sigma_A A(t)\,dW(t),
\end{align}
and suppose the firm has a single zero-coupon debt obligation of face value $D$ maturing at $T$, with default declared if $A(T) < D$. Define the \textbf{distance to default}
\begin{align}
    \mathrm{DD} = \frac{\log(A(0)/D) + \left(\mu - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_A^2\right)T}{\sigma_A\sqrt{T}}.
\end{align}
Then the \emph{physical} probability of default is $\mathbb{P}(A(T) < D) = \Phi(-\mathrm{DD})$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
    Solving the SDE, $A(T) = A(0)\exp\left[(\mu - \tfrac12\sigma_A^2)T + \sigma_A W(T)\right]$. Default occurs iff
    \begin{align}
        \log A(0) + (\mu-\tfrac12\sigma_A^2)T + \sigma_A W(T) < \log D
        \iff \frac{W(T)}{\sqrt{T}} < -\,\mathrm{DD},
    \end{align}
    and $W(T)/\sqrt{T}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$.
\end{proof}

% [CORRECTED] The original gave only the physical default probability and did
% not flag that pricing requires the risk-neutral one. Since the entire
% "market-implied vs historical" discussion later in this section turns on
% exactly this distinction, it must be made here.
\begin{remark}[Physical versus Risk-Neutral Default Probability]
    The distance to default above uses the \emph{physical} drift $\mu$ and therefore delivers the real-world default probability --- the right object for risk management, rating, and backtesting against realized default frequencies. It is \emph{not} the object used for pricing. Under the risk-neutral measure $\mathbb{Q}$, the asset drift is the risk-free rate, so the pricing-relevant quantity is
    \begin{align}
        \mathrm{DD}^{\mathbb{Q}} = \frac{\log(A(0)/D) + \left(r - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_A^2\right)T}{\sigma_A\sqrt{T}} = d_2, \qquad \mathbb{Q}(\text{default}) = \Phi(-d_2).
    \end{align}
    Since $\mu > r$ for a risky firm, $\mathrm{DD}^{\mathbb{Q}} < \mathrm{DD}$ and hence $\mathbb{Q}(\text{default}) > \mathbb{P}(\text{default})$.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Equity and Debt as Options]
    By limited liability, the equity holders receive $\max(A(T)-D,0)$, so equity is a European call on the firm's assets struck at the face value of debt:
    \begin{align}
        E(0) = A(0)\Phi(d_1) - De^{-rT}\Phi(d_2), \qquad d_1 = \frac{\log(A(0)/D)+(r+\tfrac12\sigma_A^2)T}{\sigma_A\sqrt{T}}, \quad d_2 = d_1 - \sigma_A\sqrt{T}.
    \end{align}
    Debt holders receive $\min(A(T),D) = D - \max(D-A(T),0)$, so risky debt is a risk-free bond less a put on the assets:
    \begin{align}
        B(0) = De^{-rT} - \underbrace{\left[De^{-rT}\Phi(-d_2) - A(0)\Phi(-d_1)\right]}_{\text{put = value of default option}}.
    \end{align}
    The resulting credit spread is
    \begin{align}
        s(T) = -\frac{1}{T}\log\!\left[\Phi(d_2) + \frac{A(0)}{De^{-rT}}\Phi(-d_1)\right].
    \end{align}
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}[The Calibration Problem]
    Neither $A(0)$ nor $\sigma_A$ is observable --- the firm's assets do not trade. What \emph{is} observable is equity: market capitalization $E(0)$ and equity volatility $\sigma_E$. The standard resolution, and the basis of the Moody's KMV implementation, is to solve a simultaneous system. The first equation is the option formula above; the second follows from applying It\^o's lemma to $E = E(A,t)$ and matching diffusion coefficients:
    \begin{align}
        \sigma_E E(0) = \frac{\partial E}{\partial A}\,\sigma_A A(0) = \Phi(d_1)\,\sigma_A A(0).
    \end{align}
    Two equations, two unknowns $(A(0),\sigma_A)$, solved numerically. In practice, the system is ill-conditioned for firms far from default, since $\Phi(d_1)\to 1$ and both equations become nearly degenerate; and $\sigma_E$ is itself estimated, so the procedure inherits that error. KMV additionally replaces $\Phi(-\mathrm{DD})$ by an \emph{empirical} mapping from distance to default to observed default frequency, estimated from a historical default database, precisely because the Gaussian tail is a poor description of realized default rates.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Empirical Failures]
    The Merton model has two well-known and related defects. Default can only occur \emph{at} $T$, ignoring the possibility of failure beforehand; and short-dated credit spreads are counterfactually near zero, since $\Phi(-d_2) \to 0$ faster than any power of $T$ as $T \downarrow 0$ whenever $A(0) > D$. Empirically, one-year investment-grade spreads are on the order of tens of basis points, not fractions of one. This is the ``credit spread puzzle'' in its sharpest form.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Black--Cox First-Passage Model]
    In the \textbf{Black--Cox model}, default is the first time the asset value touches a barrier,
    \begin{align}
        \tau = \inf\{t\geq 0 : A(t) \leq K(t)\}, \qquad K(t) = Ke^{\gamma(T-t)},
    \end{align}
    the exponential form being chosen so that the barrier is a fixed multiple of the discounted face value. This is a first-passage problem for geometric Brownian motion.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[First-Passage Probability]
    For a constant barrier $K < A(0)$ and asset drift $m = (\mu - \tfrac12\sigma_A^2)/\sigma_A$, writing $x_0 = \log(A(0)/K)/\sigma_A > 0$,
    \begin{align}
        \mathbb{P}(\tau \leq T) = \Phi\!\left(\frac{-x_0 - mT}{\sqrt{T}}\right) + e^{-2mx_0}\,\Phi\!\left(\frac{-x_0 + mT}{\sqrt{T}}\right).
    \end{align}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
    Taking logarithms reduces the problem to the first passage of $x_0 + mt + W(t)$ to zero. For driftless Brownian motion, the reflection principle gives $\mathbb{P}(\min_{t\leq T} W(t) \leq -x_0) = 2\Phi(-x_0/\sqrt{T})$; for $m \neq 0$, one changes measure by Girsanov, which produces the Radon--Nikodym factor $e^{-2mx_0}$ on the reflected term.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Limits of Barrier Models]
    Allowing default before maturity produces non-trivial short-dated default probabilities and materially improves the short end of the spread curve. It does not, however, eliminate the underlying problem: because $A$ has continuous paths, default remains \emph{foreseeable}, and the instantaneous spread still vanishes as $T\downarrow 0$ --- just more slowly. Genuinely non-zero short spreads require either jumps in the asset process or incomplete information about the barrier (Duffie--Lando), both of which destroy the predictability of $\tau$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Predictable versus Totally Inaccessible Stopping Times]\label{rmk:accessible}
    In a structural model with continuous asset paths, $\tau$ is a \textbf{predictable} stopping time: there exists an announcing sequence $\tau_n \uparrow \tau$ with $\tau_n < \tau$, obtained for instance by taking the first passage to barriers $K+1/n$. One sees default coming. Conditional on survival to $t$ and on $A(t)$ being strictly above the barrier, the probability of default in $[t,t+dt)$ is $o(dt)$ --- indeed exponentially small --- so the instantaneous credit spread is zero.

    In a reduced-form model $\tau$ is \textbf{totally inaccessible}: it cannot be announced by any increasing sequence of stopping times, and default arrives as a genuine surprise. The probability of default in $[t,t+dt)$ is $\lambda(t)\,dt$, of order $dt$, giving a strictly positive instantaneous spread.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Reduced-Form Models}

Reduced-form (or intensity-based) models take default as an exogenous arrival governed by a stochastic intensity.

\begin{definition}[Filtrations]
    Two filtrations must be distinguished. The \textbf{background filtration} $\mathbb{F} = (\mathcal{F}(t))$ carries all market information \emph{other} than the occurrence of default --- interest rates, macro state variables, the intensity process itself. The \textbf{default filtration} $\mathbb{H} = (\mathcal{H}(t))$ is generated by the default indicator, $\mathcal{H}(t) = \sigma(\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau\leq s\}} : s \leq t)$. The \textbf{enlarged filtration} available to an investor is $\mathbb{G} = \mathbb{F}\vee\mathbb{H}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Default Intensity]
    A non-negative $\mathbb{F}$-adapted process $\lambda(t)$ is the \textbf{default intensity} (or \textbf{hazard rate}) if
    \begin{align}
        \mathbb{P}\!\left(\tau \in [t,t+dt) \;\middle|\; \mathcal{F}(t),\, \tau > t\right) = \lambda(t)\,dt + o(dt).
    \end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Cox Process Construction]
    The canonical construction realizing a given intensity is as follows. Let $\lambda$ be a non-negative $\mathbb{F}$-adapted process and let $\mathcal{E}\sim\mathrm{Exp}(1)$ be independent of $\mathbb{F}$. Define
    \begin{align}
        \tau = \inf\left\{t \geq 0 : \int_0^t \lambda(s)\,ds \geq \mathcal{E}\right\}.
    \end{align}
    Default occurs when the accumulated hazard first exhausts an independent unit exponential ``budget.'' A default time so constructed is called \textbf{doubly stochastic}, or a \emph{Cox} default time: conditional on the whole path of $\lambda$, it is simply an inhomogeneous Poisson first arrival.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    This construction is how one simulates: draw $\mathcal{E}$, simulate the intensity path, and record the crossing time of the compensator $\int_0^t\lambda$. It makes clear that all randomness enters through two independent channels --- the state of the economy through $\lambda$, and the idiosyncratic timing through $\mathcal{E}$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Survival Probability]
    For a doubly stochastic default time with $\mathbb{F}$-adapted intensity $\lambda$,
    \begin{align}
        \mathbb{P}(\tau > T \mid \mathcal{F}(t)) = \mathbb{E}\!\left[\left. \exp\!\left(-\int_t^T \lambda(s)\,ds\right)\right| \mathcal{F}(t)\right],
    \end{align}
    and the survival probability in the investor's filtration $\mathbb{G}$ carries the indicator,
    \begin{align}
        Q(t,T) = \mathbb{P}(\tau > T\mid\mathcal{G}(t)) = \mathbb{I}_{\{\tau>t\}}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left.\exp\!\left(-\int_t^T\lambda(s)\,ds\right)\right|\mathcal{F}(t)\right].
    \end{align}
    When $\lambda$ is deterministic, this reduces to $Q(0,T) = \exp\left(-\int_0^T\lambda(s)\,ds\right)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Conditional on the entire path of $\lambda$, survival to $T$ means the exponential budget has not been exhausted: $\mathbb{P}(\tau > T \mid \mathcal{F}(T)) = \mathbb{P}\!\left(\mathcal{E} > \int_0^T\lambda\,ds \mid \mathcal{F}(T)\right) = e^{-\int_0^T\lambda\,ds}$, using independence of $\mathcal{E}$ and the survival function of a unit exponential. The result follows by the tower property. The indicator in the $\mathbb{G}$-version is required because on $\{\tau \leq t\}$ the survival probability is zero, information available in $\mathcal{G}(t)$ but not in $\mathcal{F}(t)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Compensated Default Indicator]
    Let $N(t) = \mathbb{I}_{\{\tau\leq t\}}$ be the default indicator. Then
    \begin{align}
        M(t) = N(t) - \int_0^{t\wedge\tau}\lambda(s)\,ds
    \end{align}
    is a $\mathbb{G}$-martingale, and $\int_0^{t\wedge\tau}\lambda\,ds$ is the compensator of $N$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
    This is the Doob--Meyer decomposition of the default indicator and connects directly to the compensated Poisson process of Section~9: default is a single-jump counting process, stopped at its first jump, and $\lambda$ is exactly the intensity in that sense. As there, the change of measure from $\mathbb{P}$ to $\mathbb{Q}$ acts by rescaling the intensity, $\lambda^{\mathbb{P}} \to \lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}$, which is the formal content of the risk premium discussed next.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Recovery Conventions]
    The \textbf{recovery rate} $R\in[0,1]$ is the fraction recovered at default, but the base to which it applies must be specified. Three conventions are in use:
    \begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
        \item \textbf{Recovery of face value (RFV)}: $R$ of par is paid at default. Standard for CDS, and matches the physical settlement mechanics of the contract.
        \item \textbf{Recovery of treasury (RT)}: $R$ times the value of an otherwise identical default-free bond.
        \item \textbf{Recovery of market value (RMV)}: $R$ times the bond's own pre-default market value (Duffie--Singleton).
    \end{itemize}
    In practice $R \approx 40\%$ is the conventional assumption for senior unsecured corporate debt. Realized recoveries vary enormously --- by seniority, jurisdiction, and industry --- and are strongly \emph{negatively} correlated with default rates, so recoveries are lowest precisely when defaults are most numerous. Assuming $R$ constant therefore understates tail losses systematically.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Duffie--Singleton]
    Under recovery of market value with fractional loss $1-R$, a defaultable claim prices exactly as a default-free claim discounted at the \emph{default-adjusted} short rate:
    \begin{align}
        \bar{P}(0,T) = \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}\!\left[\exp\!\left(-\int_0^T \left[r(s) + \lambda(s)(1-R)\right] ds\right)\right].
    \end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}[Credit Spread]
    The \textbf{credit spread} $s(T)$ is the yield difference between a defaultable and a default-free zero-coupon bond of the same maturity.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Credit Triangle]\label{prop:triangle}
    Under deterministic rates, deterministic intensity, and constant recovery,
    \begin{align}
        s(T) \approx (1-R)\,\frac{1}{T}\int_0^T \lambda(u)\,du,
    \end{align}
    to first order in the cumulative hazard. For constant $\lambda$ this is the \textbf{credit triangle}
    \begin{align}
        s \approx (1-R)\,\lambda \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad \lambda \approx \frac{s}{1-R}.
    \end{align}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    With recovery $R$ of face paid at maturity, $\bar{P}(0,T) = D(0,T)\left[Q + R(1-Q)\right] = D(0,T)\left[1-(1-R)(1-Q)\right]$ where $Q = e^{-\int_0^T\lambda}$. Hence
    \begin{align}
        s(T) = -\frac{1}{T}\log\!\left[1-(1-R)(1-Q)\right] \approx \frac{(1-R)(1-Q)}{T} \approx \frac{(1-R)}{T}\int_0^T\lambda(u)\,du,
    \end{align}
    using $\log(1-x)\approx -x$ and $1-Q \approx \int_0^T\lambda$, both first-order in the cumulative hazard.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Credit Default Swaps}

\begin{definition}[Credit Default Swap]
    A \textbf{credit default swap (CDS)} is a contract in which the protection buyer pays a periodic premium to the protection seller, who upon default of the reference entity pays the loss given default $(1-R)$ per unit notional. The buyer is economically short credit, holding a position analogous to a put on the reference entity's debt.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[CDS Valuation]
    Write $D(0,t)$ for the risk-free discount factor and $Q(0,t)$ for the survival probability. The \textbf{protection leg} has present value
    \begin{align}
        \mathrm{PV}_{\mathrm{prot}} = (1-R)\int_0^T D(0,t)\,\lambda(t)\,Q(0,t)\,dt,
    \end{align}
    since $\lambda(t)Q(0,t)\,dt$ is the unconditional probability of default in $[t,t+dt)$. With premium dates $t_1<\cdots<t_n=T$ and accrual factors $\Delta_i$, the \textbf{premium leg} for a contractual coupon $c$ is referred to as RPV01,
    \begin{align}
        \mathrm{PV}_{\mathrm{prem}} = c\left[\sum_{i=1}^n \Delta_i D(0,t_i)Q(0,t_i) + \int_0^T D(0,t)\,(t-t_{i(t)})\,\lambda(t)Q(0,t)\,dt\right],
    \end{align}
    where the second term is the \textbf{accrued premium} owed for the period between the last coupon date $t_{i(t)}$ and a default at $t$. The \textbf{par spread} $S$ is the coupon equating the two legs,
    \begin{align}
        S = \frac{(1-R)\int_0^T D(0,t)\lambda(t)Q(0,t)\,dt}{\mathrm{RPV01}}.
    \end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}[Risky Annuity and CS01]
    The denominator $\mathrm{RPV01}$ --- the \emph{risky annuity}, or risky PV01 --- is the present value of one basis point of premium paid until default or maturity. It is the natural numeraire of the credit market: the mark-to-market of an existing position with contractual coupon $c$ when the par spread is $S$ is simply
    \begin{align}
        V_{\text{protection buyer}} = (S - c)\times \mathrm{RPV01},
    \end{align}
    so $\mathrm{RPV01}$ converts spread changes into P\&L. The sensitivity $\mathrm{CS01} = \partial V/\partial S \approx \mathrm{RPV01}$ is the standard risk measure for a credit book, exactly analogous to DV01 for rates.
\end{remark}


\begin{example}[Hazard Rate Bootstrapping]
    Consider a 5-year CDS with $R = 40\%$, flat continuously compounded rate $r = 2\%$, and quoted par spread $S = 100$bp. Assume a constant hazard rate $\lambda$ and, for clarity, continuous premium payment with no accrual term. Then
    \begin{align}
        \underbrace{S\int_0^5 e^{-rt}e^{-\lambda t}\,dt}_{\text{premium}} = \underbrace{(1-R)\lambda\int_0^5 e^{-rt}e^{-\lambda t}\,dt}_{\text{protection}}.
    \end{align}
    The integrals are identical and cancel --- note this is exactly why the discount curve barely matters for a flat-spread bootstrap --- leaving the credit triangle
    \begin{align}
        S = (1-R)\lambda = 0.6\lambda \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \lambda = \frac{100}{0.6\times 10{,}000} = 1.667\%\text{ per year},
    \end{align}
    and hence $Q(0,5) = e^{-0.016667\times 5} = 92.0\%$: a cumulative five-year default probability of about $8.0\%$.

    In practice the term structure of quotes (1Y, 3Y, 5Y, 7Y, 10Y) is bootstrapped sequentially into a piecewise-constant hazard curve, exactly as swap rates are bootstrapped into a discount curve: solve the 1Y quote for $\lambda_{[0,1]}$, then the 3Y quote for $\lambda_{(1,3]}$ holding the first segment fixed, and so on. Upward-sloping spread curves --- the norm for investment grade --- produce upward-sloping hazard curves; inverted curves, characteristic of distressed names, imply high near-term hazard that declines if the firm survives.
\end{example}

\subsection{Portfolio Credit Risk}

Single-name models calibrate each marginal default distribution from CDS quotes. Portfolio products --- CDOs, basket default swaps, index tranches --- require the \emph{joint} distribution of default times.

\begin{theorem}[Sklar]
    Let $F$ be a joint CDF on $\mathbb{R}^d$ with marginals $F_1,\dots,F_d$. Then there exists a \textbf{copula} $C:[0,1]^d\to[0,1]$ --- itself a joint CDF with uniform marginals --- such that
    \begin{align}
        F(x_1,\dots,x_d) = C\!\left(F_1(x_1),\dots,F_d(x_d)\right).
    \end{align}
    If the marginals are continuous, $C$ is unique. Conversely, given any copula $C$ and any marginals $F_i$, the above defines a valid joint CDF.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
    Sklar's theorem is the licence for the entire copula approach: it guarantees that marginals and dependence can be specified \emph{separately} and recombined consistently. One calibrates each $F_i$ to that name's CDS curve, chooses a copula $C$ for the dependence, and constructs correlated default times as $\tau_i = F_i^{-1}(U_i)$ with $(U_1,\dots,U_d)\sim C$. The marginals are preserved exactly, by construction.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Gaussian Copula and the One-Factor Model]
    The \textbf{Gaussian copula} with correlation matrix $\Sigma$ is
    \begin{align}
        C^{\mathrm{Gauss}}_\Sigma(u_1,\dots,u_d) = \Phi_\Sigma\!\left(\Phi^{-1}(u_1),\dots,\Phi^{-1}(u_d)\right).
    \end{align}
    In the \textbf{one-factor} specialization, the latent variables are
    \begin{align}
        Z_i = \sqrt{\rho}\,X + \sqrt{1-\rho}\,\varepsilon_i, \qquad X,\varepsilon_1,\dots,\varepsilon_d \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1),
    \end{align}
    so that $\mathrm{Var}(Z_i)=1$ and $\mathrm{Corr}(Z_i,Z_j)=\rho$ for $i\neq j$. Name $i$ defaults before $T$ iff $Z_i < \Phi^{-1}(F_i(T))$. The single parameter $\rho$ governs the share of default risk attributable to the common systemic factor $X$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Conditional Independence]
    Conditional on the systemic factor $X = x$, defaults are independent, and each name's conditional default probability is
    \begin{align}
        p_i(x) = \mathbb{P}\!\left(Z_i < \Phi^{-1}(p_i) \mid X = x\right) = \Phi\!\left(\frac{\Phi^{-1}(p_i) - \sqrt{\rho}\,x}{\sqrt{1-\rho}}\right),
        \qquad p_i := F_i(T).
    \end{align}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Given $X=x$, $Z_i = \sqrt{\rho}x + \sqrt{1-\rho}\,\varepsilon_i$ depends only on $\varepsilon_i$, and the $\varepsilon_i$ are independent. Solving $Z_i < \Phi^{-1}(p_i)$ for $\varepsilon_i$ gives $\varepsilon_i < (\Phi^{-1}(p_i)-\sqrt{\rho}x)/\sqrt{1-\rho}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Collateralized Debt Obligation]
    A \textbf{CDO} redistributes portfolio losses into tranches of differing seniority. For cumulative portfolio loss $L(t)$ and a tranche with \textbf{attachment} $A$ and \textbf{detachment} $D$, the tranche loss is
    \begin{align}
        L^{A,D}(t) = \min\!\left(D-A,\;\max(L(t)-A,\,0)\right),
    \end{align}
    a call spread on the pool loss: $L^{A,D} = (L-A)^+ - (L-D)^+$. The tranche absorbs nothing until losses reach $A$ and is fully written down once they reach $D$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    Writing the tranche as a call spread on $L$ is the useful way to see it. Tranche valuation reduces to knowing $\mathbb{E}[(L-K)^+]$ for each strike $K$ --- exactly analogous to how a European option surface is determined by the marginal distribution of the underlying. Everything in tranche pricing is a statement about the loss distribution's shape, and $\rho$ is the parameter controlling that shape.
\end{remark}

% [CORRECTED] The previous example used 10 names with a 0-3% tranche. With
% LGD 60% a SINGLE default causes a 6% pool loss, which wipes out the tranche
% entirely -- the tranche is binary, never partially eroded, and so illustrates
% nothing about tranche mechanics. The stated answer of 2.1% was also wrong:
% E[L^{0,3%}] = 3% x P(at least one default) = 1.41% at rho = 0.2, and 2.1%
% exceeds even the independent-case value of 1.70%, so no rho reproduces it.
% Replaced with a 125-name CDX-style pool; all figures below verified numerically.
\begin{example}[Index Tranche Valuation]
    Take a homogeneous pool of $d = 125$ names (the size of the CDX and iTraxx indices), each with the five-year CDS spread of the previous example: $S = 100$bp, $R = 40\%$, hence $\lambda = 1.667\%$, $p = F(5) = 8.0\%$, and loss given default $1-R = 60\%$. Default of name $i$ occurs iff $Z_i < \Phi^{-1}(0.08) = -1.405$.

    Using the one-factor Gaussian copula and integrating the conditional binomial loss distribution over the systemic factor gives the following tranche expected losses, expressed as a percentage of each tranche's own notional:

    \begin{center}
    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
    \begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrl@{}}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Tranche} & $\rho = 0$ & $\rho = 0.2$ & $\rho = 0.5$ & \textbf{Exposure} \\
    \midrule
    $0$--$3\%$ (equity)      & $97.99\%$ & $75.66\%$ & $51.05\%$ & idiosyncratic \\
    $3$--$7\%$ (mezzanine)   & $45.07\%$ & $36.83\%$ & $27.93\%$ & mixed \\
    $7$--$15\%$ (senior)     & $0.68\%$  & $10.99\%$ & $14.54\%$ & systemic \\
    $15$--$100\%$ (super senior) & $0.00\%$ & $0.21\%$ & $1.16\%$ & extreme systemic \\
    \midrule
    \textbf{Whole pool} & $\mathbf{4.797\%}$ & $\mathbf{4.797\%}$ & $\mathbf{4.797\%}$ & --- \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    Two features deserve attention. First, the equity tranche expected loss \emph{falls} as correlation rises while the senior tranches' \emph{rise}: higher $\rho$ makes the ``no defaults at all'' scenario more likely (helping equity) and simultaneously makes mass simultaneous default more likely (hurting senior). The equity tranche is long correlation; senior tranches are short it. Second, the total across all tranches is $(1-R)\times p = 0.6\times 0.08 = 4.797\%$ \emph{regardless of $\rho$}.
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}[Correlation Redistributes Risk but Does Not Create It]
    For any dependence structure whatever, the expected loss of the whole pool is
    \begin{align}
        \mathbb{E}[L] = \frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^d (1-R_i)\,\mathbb{P}(\tau_i \leq T),
    \end{align}
    which depends only on the marginals. Since the tranches partition the loss, $\sum_{\text{tranches}} \mathbb{E}[L^{A,D}] = \mathbb{E}[L]$ is invariant to $\rho$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Immediate from linearity of expectation: $\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{I}_{\{\tau_i\leq T\}}]$ involves only the marginal law of $\tau_i$, and no dependence assumption enters.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    This invariance is the sharpest statement of what tranching does and does not achieve. Securitization does not reduce aggregate credit risk; it redistributes it across investors. Every basis point of expected loss removed from the equity tranche by assuming higher correlation reappears in the senior tranches. Consequently, any claim that a structure has ``created'' AAA assets from a sub-investment-grade pool is a claim about the \emph{allocation} of a fixed quantity of expected loss --- and is only as good as the correlation assumption used to allocate it.
\end{remark}


\begin{remark}[Tail Dependence and the Failure of the Gaussian Copula]
    The decisive defect of the Gaussian copula is its \textbf{tail dependence} coefficient. For any $\rho < 1$,
    \begin{align}
        \lambda_U = \lim_{u\uparrow 1}\mathbb{P}\!\left(U_2 > u \mid U_1 > u\right) = 0,
    \end{align}
    so extreme events are \emph{asymptotically independent}: conditional on one name suffering a catastrophic outcome, the probability another does too vanishes in the limit. This is a poor description of credit markets, where defaults cluster precisely in the extreme. Correlation $\rho$ controls dependence in the \emph{body} of the distribution while leaving the tail asymptotically independent --- and tranche losses are a tail phenomenon.

    The $t$-copula, obtained by replacing $\Phi$ with the multivariate $t_\nu$, has strictly positive tail dependence
    \begin{align}
        \lambda_U = 2\,t_{\nu+1}\!\left(-\sqrt{\frac{(\nu+1)(1-\rho)}{1+\rho}}\right) > 0,
    \end{align}
    which vanishes only as $\nu\to\infty$, recovering the Gaussian case. Archimedean copulas such as Clayton provide asymmetric alternatives with lower tail dependence. All of them fit senior tranches better; none is implied by the single-name data.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[What 2008 Actually Demonstrated]
    The Gaussian copula is routinely blamed for the crisis, but the failure was more specific than ``the wrong copula was used,'' and the useful lessons are these.
    \begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
        \item \textbf{The dependence parameter was never identified.} $\rho$ was calibrated to a short history of benign data, during which realized default correlation was low. Nothing in the calibration constrained behaviour in the regime that mattered.
        \item \textbf{Senior tranches are almost pure correlation bets.} As the table shows, the $15$--$100\%$ tranche moves from $0.00\%$ to $1.16\%$ expected loss as $\rho$ goes from $0$ to $0.5$ --- unbounded relative sensitivity to a parameter estimated with essentially no information. Any instrument whose value depends overwhelmingly on an unidentified parameter should be treated as a model-risk position rather than a credit position.
        \item \textbf{Correlated recovery was ignored.} Constant $R = 40\%$ understates losses systematically, since recoveries collapse exactly when defaults cluster. This compounds the tail error independently of the copula choice.
        \item \textbf{The correlation smile was visible in advance.} That no single $\rho$ could fit all tranches was known and quoted daily. The market's response was to reparametrize (base correlation) rather than to conclude the model was misspecified --- an instructive case of a quoting convention masking a modelling failure.
    \end{itemize}
    The general lesson transfers well beyond credit: when a model's output is dominated by a parameter that the calibrating data cannot identify, the resulting price is an assumption wearing the costume of a valuation.
\end{remark}
\section{Counterparty Risk and XVA}

Classical risk-neutral pricing assumes that both parties to a derivative contract will honor their obligations. In reality, counterparties can default, funding is not free, and regulatory capital must be held. The \textbf{XVA framework} adjusts the ``clean'' (risk-free) price to account for these real-world costs. The \textbf{counterparty risk} is the risk that a trading counterparty defaults before the maturity of a contract, causing a loss equal to the replacement cost of the position at the time of default.


\subsection{Exposure}

\begin{definition}
Let $V(t)$ be the mark-to-market value of a derivative portfolio from the investor's perspective. The \textbf{positive exposure} $E^+(t) = \max(V(t), 0)$ is the amount at risk if the counterparty defaults (the investor is owed money). The \textbf{negative exposure} $E^-(t) = \max(-V(t), 0)$ is the amount the investor owes.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{expected positive exposure (EPE)} and \textbf{expected negative exposure (ENE)} are
\begin{align}
    \text{EPE}(t) = \widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[E^+(t)], \qquad \text{ENE}(t) = \widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[E^-(t)].
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
The exposure profile of a derivative changes over time and depends on market conditions. For a plain vanilla interest rate swap, the EPE typically rises initially (as rates can move further from the fixed rate) and then declines toward zero as the remaining cash flows shrink. The peak of the EPE profile, called the \textbf{potential future exposure (PFE)}, is used for regulatory capital calculations.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
\textbf{Collateral agreements} (governed by Credit Support Annexes) specify the posting of \textbf{variation margin} (covering current exposure) and \textbf{initial margin} (covering potential future exposure during a close-out period). With collateral $C(t)$, the \textbf{collateralized exposure} is $\widetilde{E}(t) = V(t) - C(t)$.
\end{definition}


\subsection{Valuation Adjustments}

The separation between ``risk-free'' discounting and real-world costs of default, funding, and capital leads to a series of additive adjustments to the clean price.

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{Credit Valuation Adjustment (CVA)} is the expected loss due to counterparty default:
\begin{align}
    \text{CVA} = (1-R_c)\int_0^T D(0,t)\,\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\widetilde{E}^+(t)]\,\lambda_c(t)\,Q_c(0,t)\,dt,
\end{align}
where $R_c$ is the counterparty's recovery rate, $\lambda_c$ its default intensity, and $Q_c$ its survival probability.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
CVA is a real economic cost: if a counterparty defaults when the position has positive value to the investor, the investor loses $(1-R_c)$ times the exposure. CVA desks at major banks are profit centers that actively hedge counterparty risk by trading CDS and managing exposure profiles.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{Debit Valuation Adjustment (DVA)} accounts for the investor's own default risk:
\begin{align}
    \text{DVA} = (1-R_o)\int_0^T D(0,t)\,\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\widetilde{E}^-(t)]\,\lambda_o(t)\,Q_o(0,t)\,dt.
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
DVA is controversial. It represents the ``benefit'' the investor derives from the possibility of defaulting on obligations. This is real in an accounting sense (it reduces the investor's liabilities) but perverse from a risk management perspective (one's own creditworthiness deteriorating should not be a source of profit). Under IFRS 13, DVA must be recognized in fair value accounting, but many institutions exclude it from internal risk metrics.
\end{remark}

The remaining adjustments capture funding and regulatory costs.

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{Funding Valuation Adjustment (FVA)} is the cost of funding uncollateralized exposures at rates above the risk-free rate:
\begin{align}
    \text{FVA} = \int_0^T D(0,t)\,\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\widetilde{E}(t)]\,s_f(t)\,dt,
\end{align}
where $s_f(t)$ is the funding spread. The \textbf{Capital Valuation Adjustment (KVA)} is the cost of holding regulatory capital:
\begin{align}
    \text{KVA} = \int_0^T D(0,t)\,\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[K(t)]\,h\,dt,
\end{align}
where $K(t)$ is the required capital and $h$ is the hurdle rate. The \textbf{Margin Valuation Adjustment (MVA)} is the funding cost of posting initial margin:
\begin{align}
    \text{MVA} = \int_0^T D(0,t)\,\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\text{IM}(t)]\,s_f(t)\,dt.
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
The total value of a derivative portfolio, including all adjustments, is
\begin{align}
    V_{\text{total}} = V_{\text{clean}} - \text{CVA} + \text{DVA} - \text{FVA} - \text{KVA} - \text{MVA}.
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
Each adjustment has a sign: CVA, FVA, KVA, and MVA reduce value (they are costs), while DVA increases it (it is a benefit from the investor's perspective). The magnitudes can be substantial: for a large uncollateralized swap portfolio, CVA alone can be several percent of notional.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Wrong-Way Risk and Practical Considerations}

\begin{definition}
\textbf{Wrong-way risk (WWR)} arises when exposure and counterparty credit quality are positively correlated:
\begin{align}
    \text{Corr}(E^+(t), \lambda_c(t)) > 0.
\end{align}
When the counterparty is most likely to default, the exposure is largest.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[Wrong-way risk in practice]
Consider a bank that has sold CDS protection to a hedge fund referencing the bank's own sector. If the sector deteriorates, the bank's exposure (it owes money on the CDS) increases at the same time that the hedge fund's creditworthiness worsens (it is a leveraged player in the same sector). The factorized CVA formula $\text{CVA} \approx (1-R)\,\text{EPE} \times \text{PD}$ dramatically underestimates the loss, because it treats exposure and default probability as independent.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
Wrong-way risk invalidates the standard factorized CVA formula and requires joint modeling of market risk factors and counterparty credit quality. This requires simulating both market scenarios and credit scenarios on the same paths.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{netting set} is a collection of trades with a single counterparty governed by a legally enforceable netting agreement. Upon default, exposures within the netting set are aggregated before close-out: positive and negative values offset, reducing the net exposure. Netting can dramatically reduce CVA.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[XVA for an interest rate swap]
Consider a 5-year payer swap with notional \$100M, fixed rate 2\%, discounting at $r = 1\%$. Assume deterministic exposure profiles:
\begin{align}
    \text{EPE}(t) = 5\,e^{-0.05t} \text{ (\$M)}, \qquad \text{ENE}(t) = 2\,e^{-0.05t} \text{ (\$M)}.
\end{align}
Counterparty: $R_c = 40\%$, $\lambda_c = 1\%$/yr. Investor: $R_o = 40\%$, $\lambda_o = 0.5\%$/yr.

The CVA is
\begin{align}
    \text{CVA} &= 0.6 \times 0.01 \times 5\int_0^5 e^{-(0.01+0.05+0.01)t}\,dt 
    \\
    &= 0.03\int_0^5 e^{-0.07t}\,dt \approx 0.03 \times 4.21 \approx \$126\text{K}.
\end{align}
The DVA is
\begin{align}
    \text{DVA} &= 0.6 \times 0.005 \times 2\int_0^5 e^{-(0.01+0.05+0.005)t}\,dt \approx 0.006 \times 4.27 \approx \$26\text{K}.
\end{align}
If the clean value is \$800K:
\begin{align}
    V_{\text{total}} = 800 - 126 + 26 = \$700\text{K}.
\end{align}
The CVA alone reduces the portfolio value by 16\%.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
In practice, exposure profiles are stochastic (computed via Monte Carlo simulation of interest rates, FX, and credit jointly), collateral dynamics are path-dependent (margin calls have thresholds and delays), and netting across trade types introduces complex optionality.
\end{remark}


\chapter{Portfolio Management}

The preceding chapters priced individual derivatives by constructing hedges and computing risk-neutral expectations. Portfolio management asks a different question: given many available assets, how should an investor allocate capital? The answer depends on the investor's objectives (return, risk tolerance, benchmark tracking) and constraints (leverage limits, regulatory requirements, liquidity needs).

This chapter develops two complementary perspectives. First, \textbf{portfolio theory} solves the allocation problem: mean-variance optimization identifies the best trade-off between expected return and risk, the CAPM links individual asset pricing to market equilibrium, and factor models decompose returns into systematic and idiosyncratic components. Second, \textbf{portfolio risk} provides the quantitative tools for measuring and controlling downside exposure: Value at Risk, Expected Shortfall, drawdowns, diversification, and hedging.


\section{Portfolio Theory}

\subsection{Mean-Variance Analysis}

The Markowitz framework reduces portfolio selection to a quadratic optimization problem. Despite its simplicity, it remains the foundation for more elaborate models and is the starting point for practical portfolio construction.

\begin{definition}
Consider a single-period economy with $m$ risky assets and one risk-free asset. Let the random return vector on the risky assets be $\vec{R} = (R_1, \ldots, R_m)$ with expected returns $\vec{\mu} = \mathbb{E}[\vec{R}]$ and covariance matrix $\Sigma = \text{Cov}(\vec{R})$, assumed symmetric and positive definite. The risk-free return is $r_0$.

A portfolio is specified by weight vector $\vec{w} = (w_1, \ldots, w_m)$, where $w_i$ is the fraction of wealth invested in risky asset $i$. The risk-free weight is $w_0 = 1 - \vec{w} \cdot \vec{1}_m$ (negative if borrowing). The \textbf{portfolio return} is
\begin{align}
    R_p = \vec{w} \cdot \vec{R} + (1 - \vec{w} \cdot \vec{1}_m)\,r_0.
\end{align}
Under the \textbf{Markowitz model}, the portfolio's mean and variance are
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}[R_p] = \vec{w} \cdot \vec{\mu} + (1 - \vec{w} \cdot \vec{1}_m)\,r_0, \qquad \text{Var}(R_p) = \vec{w}^\top \Sigma\, \vec{w}.
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
The variance $\vec{w}^\top \Sigma\, \vec{w}$ depends only on the risky weights. The expected return is linear in $\vec{w}$, while the variance is quadratic. This asymmetry is what makes the optimization tractable: we are maximizing a linear function subject to a quadratic penalty.
\end{remark}

To make the trade-off between return and risk precise, we need a preference specification.

\begin{definition}
A rational investor has an increasing, concave utility function $U$. The \textbf{portfolio choice problem} is
\begin{align}
    \max_{\vec{w}}\;\mathbb{E}\bigl[U(1 + R_p)\bigr].
\end{align}
For \textbf{power utility} with constant relative risk aversion $\gamma > 0$,
\begin{align}
    U(x) = \frac{x^{1-\gamma}}{1-\gamma},
\end{align}
a second-order Taylor expansion shows that maximizing expected utility is approximately equivalent to the \textbf{mean-variance problem}:
\begin{align}
    \max_{\vec{w}}\;\Bigl(\mathbb{E}[R_p] - \frac{\gamma}{2}\,\text{Var}(R_p)\Bigr).
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
The parameter $\gamma$ controls the investor's willingness to bear risk. A pension fund with $\gamma \approx 5$--$10$ takes moderate positions; a leveraged hedge fund operating with $\gamma \approx 1$--$2$ takes aggressive ones. The mean-variance approximation is exact when returns are Gaussian or the utility is quadratic; otherwise it is a convenient approximation that becomes less accurate for large positions or heavy-tailed distributions.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Optimal portfolio]
The solution to the mean-variance problem is
\begin{align}
    \vec{w}^* = \frac{1}{\gamma}\,\Sigma^{-1}(\vec{\mu} - r_0\,\vec{1}_m),
\end{align}
with the remaining wealth $1 - \vec{w}^* \cdot \vec{1}_m$ invested in the risk-free asset.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
The objective is $\vec{w} \cdot (\vec{\mu} - r_0\,\vec{1}_m) + r_0 - \frac{\gamma}{2}\vec{w}^\top\Sigma\,\vec{w}$, which is concave in $\vec{w}$. Taking the gradient and setting it to zero: $\vec{\mu} - r_0\,\vec{1}_m - \gamma\Sigma\,\vec{w} = 0$, which gives the stated formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The optimal portfolio is proportional to $\Sigma^{-1}(\vec{\mu} - r_0\,\vec{1}_m)$: the inverse covariance matrix ``decorrelates'' the assets, and the excess return vector determines the direction. More risk-averse investors ($\gamma$ large) hold smaller positions in the same proportions.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Two-asset portfolio]
Consider two risky assets with $\mu_1 = 8\%$, $\mu_2 = 12\%$, $\sigma_1 = 15\%$, $\sigma_2 = 25\%$, $\rho_{12} = 0.3$, and $r_0 = 3\%$. The covariance matrix is
\begin{align}
    \Sigma = \begin{pmatrix} 0.0225 & 0.01125 \\ 0.01125 & 0.0625 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad
    \Sigma^{-1} = \begin{pmatrix} 51.28 & -9.23 \\ -9.23 & 18.46 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{align}
The excess return vector is $\vec{\mu} - r_0\vec{1} = (0.05, 0.09)^\top$. For $\gamma = 3$:
\begin{align}
    \vec{w}^* = \frac{1}{3}\begin{pmatrix} 51.28 \times 0.05 - 9.23 \times 0.09 \\ -9.23 \times 0.05 + 18.46 \times 0.09 \end{pmatrix} = \frac{1}{3}\begin{pmatrix} 1.733 \\ 1.200 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0.578 \\ 0.400 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{align}
The investor puts 57.8\% in asset 1, 40.0\% in asset 2, and $1 - 0.978 = 2.2\%$ in the risk-free asset. The portfolio is nearly fully invested. With $\gamma = 10$, the weights would be $(0.173, 0.120)$, with 70.7\% in the risk-free asset.
\end{example}

The set of all optimal portfolios, as $\gamma$ varies, traces a straight line in mean-standard-deviation space.

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{tangency portfolio} is the unique efficient portfolio fully invested in risky assets ($\vec{w} \cdot \vec{1}_m = 1$). It corresponds to risk aversion
\begin{align}
    \hat{\gamma} = \vec{1}_m^\top \Sigma^{-1}(\vec{\mu} - r_0\,\vec{1}_m),
\end{align}
with weights $\hat{\vec{w}} = \hat{\gamma}^{-1}\Sigma^{-1}(\vec{\mu} - r_0\,\vec{1}_m)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Two-fund separation]
Every optimal portfolio can be written as a combination of the risk-free asset and the tangency portfolio:
\begin{align}
    \vec{w} = \frac{\hat{\gamma}}{\gamma}\,\hat{\vec{w}}.
\end{align}
The fraction $\hat{\gamma}/\gamma$ invested in the tangency portfolio determines the risk level. In mean-standard-deviation space, all optimal portfolios lie on the \textbf{capital allocation line (CAL)}:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}[R_p] = r_0 + \frac{\mathbb{E}[\hat{R}_p] - r_0}{\sqrt{\text{Var}(\hat{R}_p)}}\,\sqrt{\text{Var}(R_p)}.
\end{align}
The slope is the \textbf{Sharpe ratio} of the tangency portfolio.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
Regardless of their risk aversion, all investors ''ought to'' hold the same tangency portfolio. They differ only in how much of it they hold (the rest is in the risk-free asset). This is the mathematical justification for index investing: if all investors agree on $\vec{\mu}$ and $\Sigma$, the tangency portfolio is the market portfolio.

In practice, two-fund separation breaks down because investors disagree on expected returns, face different constraints (taxes, regulatory, ESG), and because $\vec{\mu}$ and $\Sigma$ are estimated with error. The sensitivity of $\vec{w}^* = \gamma^{-1}\Sigma^{-1}(\vec{\mu} - r_0\vec{1})$ to estimation error in $\vec{\mu}$ is severe, wherein small changes in expected returns produce large changes in optimal weights. This is the ``Markowitz curse.''
\end{remark}

\subsection{Factor Models and Performance Evaluation}

Mean-variance analysis takes expected returns and covariances as given. Factor models explain where those returns come from, decomposing asset risk into systematic (market-wide) and idiosyncratic (asset-specific) components.

\begin{theorem}[Capital Asset Pricing Model]
Assume all investors are mean-variance optimizers with homogeneous beliefs, a risk-free asset exists, and markets clear. Let $R_m$ be the return on the \textbf{market portfolio} (the value-weighted portfolio of all risky assets). Then, in equilibrium, the expected excess return on any asset $i$ satisfies
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}[R_i - r_0] = \beta_i\,\mathbb{E}[R_m - r_0], \qquad \beta_i = \frac{\text{Cov}(R_i, R_m)}{\text{Var}(R_m)}.
\end{align}
Only systematic risk (measured by $\beta_i$) is compensated; idiosyncratic risk earns no premium because it can be diversified away.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
The CAPM is a single-factor model: the market portfolio is the sole risk factor. It explains the broad cross-section of returns (high-beta stocks earn more on average than low-beta stocks) but leaves significant unexplained variation. This motivates multi-factor extensions.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{multi-factor model} specifies
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}[R_i - r_0] = \sum_{j=1}^J \beta_{i,j}\,\mathbb{E}[F_j],
\end{align}
where $F_1, \ldots, F_J$ are systematic risk factors with $\beta_{i,j} = \text{Cov}(R_i, F_j)/\text{Var}(F_j)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
The most widely used multi-factor model in empirical finance is the \textbf{Fama-French three-factor model}, which adds size (SMB: small minus big) and value (HML: high book-to-market minus low) to the market factor. Extensions include momentum (Carhart four-factor), profitability and investment (Fama-French five-factor), and macro factors (term spread, credit spread, inflation). In industry, proprietary factor models (e.g., Barra, Axioma) use dozens of factors and are the basis for risk decomposition, performance attribution, and portfolio construction.
\end{remark}

Factor models provide the framework for evaluating portfolio performance.

\begin{definition}
Given a factor model, the time-series regression for fund $i$ is
\begin{align}
    R_{i,t} - r_{0,t} = \alpha_i + \sum_{j=1}^J \beta_{i,j}\,F_{j,t} + \varepsilon_{i,t}.
\end{align}
The intercept $\alpha_i$ (\textbf{alpha}) measures abnormal performance not explained by factor exposures (the manager's ``skill''). The coefficients $\beta_{i,j}$ (\textbf{betas}) measure the portfolio's systematic exposures.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{information ratio} is
\begin{align}
    \text{IR}_i = \frac{\alpha_i}{\sigma(\varepsilon_{i,t})},
\end{align}
measuring risk-adjusted abnormal return per unit of idiosyncratic (non-factor) risk. An IR above 0.5 is considered good; above 1.0 is exceptional and rare over sustained periods.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[Performance attribution]
A long/short equity fund reports annual excess return of 9\%. Running the Fama-French regression yields $\beta_{\text{MKT}} = 0.2$ (low market exposure), $\beta_{\text{SMB}} = 0.5$ (tilted toward small caps), $\beta_{\text{HML}} = -0.3$ (tilted toward growth), and $\alpha = 4.5\%$. If the factor premia over the period were $\text{MKT} = 8\%$, $\text{SMB} = 3\%$, $\text{HML} = 2\%$, the decomposition is:
\begin{align}
    9\% = \underbrace{4.5\%}_{\alpha} + \underbrace{0.2 \times 8\%}_{1.6\%} + \underbrace{0.5 \times 3\%}_{1.5\%} + \underbrace{(-0.3) \times 2\%}_{-0.6\%} + \underbrace{\varepsilon}_{2.0\%}.
\end{align}
Half of the return came from alpha (stock selection), with the rest from factor tilts and residual. An investor paying 2-and-20 fees for this fund is paying for the 4.5\% alpha, whereas the factor returns could be obtained cheaply via ETFs.
\end{example}

\section{Covariance Matrices}
 
Mean-variance optimization is quite sensitive to the covariance matrix $\Sigma$. In practice, $\Sigma$ must be estimated from a finite sample of returns. When the number of assets $m$ is comparable to the number of observations $T$ (a ubiquitous regime in portfolio management), the sample
covariance matrix $\hat{\Sigma}$ becomes a poor estimator: its smallest eigenvalues are systematically underestimated, its largest systematically overestimated, and plugging it into the optimal weight formula $\vec{w}^* = \gamma^{-1}\Sigma^{-1}(\vec{\mu}-r_0\vec{1})$ amplifies estimation error catastrophically. Contemporary random matrix theory methods provide ways to innovate around this.
 
\subsection{Sample Covariance}
 
\begin{definition}
Let $\{R_t\}_{t=1}^T$ be a time series of $m$-dimensional return vectors with true covariance $\Sigma$. The \textbf{sample covariance matrix} is
\begin{align}
    \hat{\Sigma} = \frac{1}{T-1}\sum_{t=1}^T (R_t - \bar{R})(R_t - \bar{R})^\top,
    \qquad \bar{R} = \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^T R_t.
\end{align}
Let $c = m/T$ denote the \textbf{concentration ratio}.
\end{definition}
 
\begin{remark}
When $c \ll 1$ (many observations per asset), $\hat{\Sigma}$ is a good estimator. But in practice, with $m = 500$ stocks and $T = 250$ trading days, $c = 2 > 1$: the matrix is singular and cannot even be inverted. The regime $c \sim 1$, so $m$ and $T$ comparable, is the relevant one for institutional portfolios, and it is here that RMT results are most powerful.
\end{remark}
 
\begin{theorem}[Marchenko--Pastur law]
Let $X$ be a $T \times m$ matrix with i.i.d.\ entries of mean zero and variance $\sigma^2$. As $m, T \to \infty$ with $m/T \to c \in (0,\infty)$, the empirical spectral distribution of the sample covariance matrix $(1/T)X^T X$ converges almost surely to the \textbf{Marchenko--Pastur distribution} with density
\begin{align}
    \rho_{\mathrm{MP}}(\lambda) =
    \frac{\sqrt{(\lambda_+ - \lambda)(\lambda - \lambda_-)}}{2\pi c \sigma^2 \lambda}
    \cdot \mathbb{I}_{[\lambda_-, \lambda_+]}(\lambda),
\end{align}
where
\begin{align}
    \lambda_\pm = \sigma^2(1 \pm \sqrt{c})^2
\end{align}
are the upper and lower bulk edges.
\end{theorem}
 
\begin{remark}[Proof sketch]
The Stieltjes transform $g(z) = \int(\lambda - z)^{-1}\rho(\lambda)\,d\lambda$ satisfies a fixed-point equation in the large-$m$ limit, derivable via the moment method or free probability. Inverting the Stieltjes transform yields the stated density.
\end{remark}
 
\begin{remark}
The Marchenko--Pastur law is the ``null distribution'' of eigenvalues: if returns had no genuine pairwise correlation, the entire empirical spectrum of $\hat{\Sigma}/\sigma^2$ would lie within $[\lambda_-,\lambda_+]$. Eigenvalues
outside this bulk therefore carry genuine signal. For equity returns with $c \approx 0.5$--$2$, the bulk typically extends from near zero up to $\lambda_+ \approx \sigma^2(1+\sqrt{c})^2$. In practice, the largest few
eigenvalues (associated with market, sector, and style factors) sit far above $\lambda_+$, while the hundreds of remaining eigenvalues are indistinguishable
from noise.
\end{remark}
 
\begin{example}[Bulk edge for a U.S.\ equity portfolio]
Consider a portfolio of $m = 400$ stocks estimated from $T = 500$ daily returns ($c = 0.8$), with average daily variance $\sigma^2 = (0.02)^2 = 4\times 10^{-4}$. The Marchenko--Pastur bulk edges are
\begin{align}
    \lambda_\pm = 4\times10^{-4}\,(1 \pm \sqrt{0.8})^2
    \;\Rightarrow\;
    \lambda_+ \approx 1.45\times10^{-3},\quad
    \lambda_- \approx 1.75\times10^{-5}.
\end{align}
Any sample eigenvalue between $1.75\times10^{-5}$ and $1.45\times10^{-3}$ is consistent with pure noise; only eigenvalues above $1.45\times10^{-3}$ carry information about genuine covariance structure.
\end{example}
 
\subsection{Eigenvalue Cleaning and Shrinkage}
 
Two broad strategies address the noise in $\hat{\Sigma}$: eigenvalue cleaning (which applies RMT directly) and shrinkage (which penalizes departure from a structured target).
 
\begin{definition}
\textbf{Eigenvalue cleaning} (Laloux et al., 1999; Plerou et al., 2002) proceeds as follows. Let $\hat{\Sigma} = \sum_{k=1}^m \hat{\lambda}_k \hat{v}_k \hat{v}_k^\top$ be the spectral decomposition of the sample covariance matrix. Classify each eigenvalue:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item If $\hat{\lambda}_k > \lambda_+$: the eigenvector carries signal; retain
          $\hat{\lambda}_k$.
    \item If $\hat{\lambda}_- \leq \hat{\lambda}_k \leq \lambda_+$: the
          eigenvector is noise; replace $\hat{\lambda}_k$ with a constant
          $\bar{\lambda}$, chosen so that the total variance is preserved.
\end{enumerate}
The \textbf{cleaned covariance matrix} is
\begin{align}
    \hat{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{clean}} = \sum_{k:\,\hat{\lambda}_k > \lambda_+}
    \hat{\lambda}_k \hat{v}_k \hat{v}_k^\top
    + \bar{\lambda}\sum_{k:\,\hat{\lambda}_k \leq \lambda_+}
    \hat{v}_k \hat{v}_k^\top.
\end{align}
\end{definition}
 
\begin{remark}
The preservation constraint $\operatorname{tr}(\hat{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{clean}}) =
\operatorname{tr}(\hat{\Sigma})$ determines $\bar{\lambda}$. The cleaned matrix has significantly larger condition number than the raw $\hat{\Sigma}$, making portfolio optimization far less sensitive to estimation error.
\end{remark}
 
\begin{theorem}[Oracle estimator and the Ledoit--Wolf shrinkage]
Among all estimators of the form $\hat{\Sigma}_\alpha = (1-\alpha)F + \alpha\hat{\Sigma}$, where $F$ is a structured \textbf{shrinkage target} (e.g., a constant-correlation
matrix or a scaled identity), the optimal shrinkage intensity minimizing expected Frobenius loss is
\begin{align}
    \alpha^* = \frac{\|\hat{\Sigma} - F\|_F^2 + \operatorname{tr}(\hat{\Sigma}^2)/T
    - (1 + 1/T)\|\hat{\Sigma}\|_F^2/T}
    {\|\hat{\Sigma} - F\|_F^2}.
\end{align}
Under the Marchenko--Pastur asymptotics ($m,T\to\infty$, $c\to c_0$), a closed-form \textbf{analytical shrinkage} formula replaces this with a consistent estimator of the oracle intensity.
\end{theorem}
 
\begin{remark}[Proof sketch]
Expand $\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{\Sigma}_\alpha - \Sigma\|_F^2]$ in $\alpha$, use the Stein identity for the gradient of the expected loss, and minimize. The Ledoit--Wolf (2004) result provides an explicit, fully data-driven estimate of
$\alpha^*$ that requires no choice of tuning parameters. Their later (2020) ``analytical nonlinear shrinkage'' goes further: instead of a single scalar $\alpha$, it applies an optimal nonlinear transformation to each eigenvalue
using the Hilbert transform of the empirical spectral distribution, achieving the oracle Frobenius risk asymptotically.
\end{remark}
 
\begin{proposition}[Factor model regularization]
Assume returns follow a $K$-factor model: $R_t = Bf_t + \varepsilon_t$, where $B$ is $m\times K$ with $K \ll m$, the factors $f_t$ have covariance $\Phi$, and the idiosyncratic terms have diagonal covariance $D = \operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1^2,\ldots,\sigma_m^2)$. Then
\begin{align}
    \Sigma = B\Phi B^\top + D,
    \qquad
    \Sigma^{-1} = D^{-1} - D^{-1}B(\Phi^{-1} + B^\top D^{-1}B)^{-1}B^\top D^{-1},
\end{align}
where the second identity is the Woodbury formula. Estimating $B$, $\Phi$, and $D$ separately (via PCA or maximum likelihood) requires estimating only $\mathcal{O}(mK + K^2 + m)$ parameters rather than $\mathcal{O}(m^2)$, a massive reduction.
\end{proposition}
 
\begin{remark}
This is the foundation of risk models such as Barra and Axioma. The leading $K$ eigenvectors of $\hat{\Sigma}$ (those with eigenvalues above $\lambda_+$) serve as the estimated factor loadings $B$. The residual diagonal $\hat{D}$ is read
off from the remaining variance. The factor model covariance is then immediately invertible via Woodbury, regardless of $m$ and $T$.
\end{remark}
 
\begin{example}[Impact on portfolio weights]
Consider two estimators applied to the same 400-stock, 500-day dataset: the raw $\hat{\Sigma}$ and the Ledoit--Wolf shrinkage $\hat{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{LW}}$. The optimal weights $\vec{w}^* \propto \hat{\Sigma}^{-1}(\hat{\mu} - r_0\vec{1})$
computed with the raw matrix are dominated by the smallest estimated eigenvalues (pure noise directions that appear artificially low in risk). The resulting portfolio has extreme long-short positions in individual stocks and
massive turnover.
 
The shrinkage-based weights $\hat{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{LW}}^{-1}(\hat{\mu} - r_0\vec{1})$ are far more stable: the shrinkage lifts the noise eigenvalues, reducing the effective leverage and producing a portfolio whose out-of-sample Sharpe ratio is significantly higher. This is the ``Markowitz curse'' in action and its remedy.
\end{example}
 
\section{Stochastic Optimal Control}
 
Mean-variance optimization is a static, single-period framework. The richer problem of continuously rebalancing a portfolio over an infinite or finite horizon, in the presence of stochastic investment opportunities, is a problem of
\textbf{stochastic optimal control}. This section develops the
Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman (HJB) framework and applies it to Merton's continuous-time portfolio problem. The Almgren--Chriss execution problem of the next chapter will appear as a special case.
 
\subsection{Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman}
 
\begin{definition}
Let $X(t) \in \mathbb{R}^n$ be a controlled Markov diffusion:
\begin{align}
    dX(t) = b(t, X(t), u(t))\,dt + \sigma(t, X(t), u(t))\,dW(t),
\end{align}
where $u(t)$ is an adapted \textbf{control process} taking values in a set $\mathcal{U}$. The agent seeks to maximize the \textbf{value function}
\begin{align}
    V(t,x) = \sup_{u \in \mathcal{U}} \mathbb{E}\!\left[\int_t^T
    f(s, X(s), u(s))\,ds + g(X(T))\;\middle|\;X(t) = x\right],
\end{align}
where $f$ is the running reward and $g$ is the terminal reward.
\end{definition}
 
\begin{theorem}[Dynamic programming principle]
Under standard regularity conditions, the value function satisfies
\begin{align}
    V(t,x) = \sup_{u \in \mathcal{U}}\;\mathbb{E}\!\left[\int_t^{t+h}
    f(s,X(s),u(s))\,ds + V(t+h, X(t+h))\;\middle|\;X(t) = x\right]
\end{align}
for all $h > 0$. In words: the optimal value at $(t,x)$ is obtained by choosing the control optimally over a small interval $[t,t+h]$ and then proceeding optimally from the resulting state.
\end{theorem}
 
\begin{remark}[Bellman's principle]
This is Bellman's principle of optimality: a suffix of an optimal trajectory is itself optimal. It converts a global optimization over functions into a recursive local condition, just as the Markov property converts global path integrals into local generators.
\end{remark}
 
\begin{theorem}[Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman equation]
If $V \in C^{1,2}([0,T) \times \mathbb{R}^n)$, then $V$ satisfies the
\textbf{HJB equation}
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial V}{\partial t} + \sup_{u \in \mathcal{U}}\bigl[\mathcal{L}^u V
    + f(t,x,u)\bigr] = 0,
    \qquad V(T,x) = g(x),
\end{align}
where the controlled generator is
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{L}^u V = b(t,x,u) \cdot \nabla_x V + \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr}\!\bigl[\sigma\sigma^\top(t,x,u)\,\nabla^2_x V\bigr].
\end{align}
If a smooth solution $V$ exists and the supremum is attained by some $u^*(t,x)$, then $u^*$ is the optimal feedback control.
\end{theorem}
 
\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
Apply It\^o's lemma to $V(t, X(t))$:
\begin{align}
    dV = \bigl[V_t + \mathcal{L}^u V\bigr]dt + \nabla_x V \cdot \sigma\,dW.
\end{align}
Integrating from $t$ to $t+h$, taking conditional expectations (the stochastic integral vanishes), dividing by $h$, and using the dynamic programming principle yields $V_t + \sup_u[\mathcal{L}^u V + f] = 0$ in the limit $h\to 0$.
\end{proof}
 
\begin{remark}
The HJB equation is a nonlinear PDE backward in time with a terminal condition. Its solution is generically difficult: the supremum introduces nonlinearity, and the curse of dimensionality plagues high-dimensional state spaces. For affine control problems (where $b$ is affine in $u$ and $f$ is quadratic in $u$), the HJB reduces to a Riccati equation with explicit solutions.
\end{remark}
 
\subsection{Merton's Portfolio Problem}
 
The most celebrated application of stochastic control in finance is Merton's
(1969, 1971) continuous-time portfolio choice problem.
 
\begin{definition}
An investor allocates wealth $X(t)$ between $m$ risky assets and a risk-free asset at rate $r$. The fraction of wealth in risky asset $i$ is $\pi_i(t)$ (with $1-\sum_i\pi_i$ in the risk-free asset). Under risk-neutral dynamics, the wealth process satisfies
\begin{align}
    dX(t) = X(t)\Bigl[r + \pi(t)^\top(\mu - r\mathbf{1})\Bigr]dt
    + X(t)\pi(t)^\top\sigma\,dW(t),
\end{align}
where $\mu - r\mathbf{1}$ is the vector of excess returns and $\sigma$ the Cholesky factor of $\Sigma$. The investor maximizes expected utility of terminal wealth:
\begin{align}
    V(t,x) = \sup_{\pi} \mathbb{E}\bigl[U(X(T))\;\big|\;X(t) = x\bigr].
\end{align}
\end{definition}
 
\begin{theorem}[Merton's solution for power utility]
For $U(x) = x^{1-\gamma}/(1-\gamma)$ with $\gamma > 0$, $\gamma \neq 1$, the value function takes the separable form $V(t,x) = h(t)\,x^{1-\gamma}/(1-\gamma)$ where $h(t)$ solves an ODE. The optimal portfolio policy is the \textbf{constant Merton ratio}
\begin{align}
    \pi^* = \frac{1}{\gamma}\Sigma^{-1}(\mu - r\mathbf{1}),
\end{align}
and the resulting optimal wealth has dynamics
\begin{align}
    dX^*(t) = X^*(t)\Bigl[r + \frac{1}{\gamma}(\mu-r\mathbf{1})^\top
    \Sigma^{-1}(\mu-r\mathbf{1})\Bigr]dt
    + X^*(t)\frac{1}{\gamma}(\mu-r\mathbf{1})^\top\Sigma^{-1}\sigma\,dW(t).
\end{align}
\end{theorem}
 
\begin{proof}
Substitute the ansatz $V = h(t)\,x^{1-\gamma}/(1-\gamma)$ into the HJB equation. The supremum over $\pi$ is a concave quadratic, attained at $\pi^* = \frac{1}{\gamma}\Sigma^{-1}(\mu-r\mathbf{1})$, independent of $(t,x)$. Substituting back yields an ODE for $h(t)$ with terminal condition $h(T) = 1$, which integrates explicitly.
\end{proof}
 
\begin{remark}
The optimal portfolio $\pi^*$ is ''myopic'': it is identical to the single-period mean-variance optimum. This is a special property of power utility; with stochastic investment opportunities (time-varying $\mu$ or $\Sigma$), an
intertemporal hedging demand arises. Second, the fraction of wealth held in each risky asset is constant, so the strategy requires continuous rebalancing. Third, $1/\gamma$ plays the role of risk aversion: a more risk-averse investor
($\gamma$ large) holds less of the risky portfolio, confirming the two-fund separation.
\end{remark}
 
\begin{example}[Verification argument]
The smooth solution $V$ of the HJB equation is not automatically the true value function. The \textbf{verification theorem} establishes this: if $\tilde{V}
\in C^{1,2}$ satisfies the HJB with terminal condition $g$, and the optimal feedback control $u^*(t,x) = \operatorname{argmax}_u[\mathcal{L}^u \tilde{V} + f]$ produces a well-posed controlled SDE, then $\tilde{V} = V$.
 
For Merton's problem: let $\tilde{V} = h(t)x^{1-\gamma}/(1-\gamma)$. The controlled process $X^*(t)$ is log-normal and integrable; the stochastic integral in the It\^o expansion of $\tilde{V}(t,X^*(t))$ is a true martingale (not merely a local one); and the ODE for $h$ integrates to a bounded
function on $[0,T]$. These are the three conditions needed to close the verification.
\end{example}
 
\begin{remark}[Extensions]
Merton's framework extends in several directions that are active areas of research. \textbf{Stochastic opportunity sets} (where $\mu$ or $\Sigma$ follow their own diffusion) introduce hedging demands for the state variables, leading
to multi-factor HJB equations. \textbf{Constraints} (no shorting, leverage limits) truncate the feasible set for $\pi$, and the supremum in the HJB must be taken over the constrained set, often requiring numerical PDE solvers.
\textbf{Transaction costs} make the control problem degenerate: trading is costly, so the optimal policy is to do nothing inside a ``no-trade region'' and rebalance to the boundary otherwise, which gives a free-boundary problem paralleling the American option problem of the previous chapter.
\end{remark}

\section{Portfolio Risk}

Portfolio theory tells us how to allocate capital; risk management tells us how to control the downside.

\subsection{Risk Measures}

\begin{definition}
Let $L$ be a random variable representing the \textbf{loss} of a portfolio over a fixed horizon, where positive values correspond to losses. The distribution of $L$ summarizes the portfolio's risk profile.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
For a confidence level $\alpha \in (0,1)$, the \textbf{Value at Risk (VaR)} at level $\alpha$ is the $\alpha$-quantile of the loss distribution:
\begin{align}
    \text{VaR}_\alpha(L) = \inf\bigl\{\ell \in \mathbb{R} : \mathbb{P}(L \leq \ell) \geq \alpha\bigr\}.
\end{align}
With probability $\alpha$, the loss does not exceed $\text{VaR}_\alpha$ over the given horizon.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[VaR computation]
A portfolio has normally distributed daily P\&L with mean \$0 and standard deviation \$1M. The 99\% VaR is $\text{VaR}_{0.99} = 2.326 \times \$1\text{M} = \$2.326\text{M}$: with 99\% probability, the daily loss does not exceed \$2.33M. Equivalently, losses exceeding \$2.33M are expected about 2--3 times per year (1\% of 252 trading days $\approx 2.5$).
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
VaR is simple, intuitive, and widely mandated by regulators (Basel III/IV). However, it has a fundamental theoretical flaw: VaR is not \textbf{subadditive}. That is, it is possible to have $\text{VaR}(L_1 + L_2) > \text{VaR}(L_1) + \text{VaR}(L_2)$, wherein combining two portfolios can appear to increase risk, even when diversification should reduce it. This pathology occurs for heavy-tailed distributions and makes VaR unsuitable as a basis for capital allocation across desks.
\end{remark}

Expected Shortfall fixes this problem.

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{Expected Shortfall (ES)}, or \textbf{Conditional Value at Risk (CVaR)}, at level $\alpha$ is
\begin{align}
    \text{ES}_\alpha(L) = \mathbb{E}\bigl[L \mid L \geq \text{VaR}_\alpha(L)\bigr].
\end{align}
It measures the expected loss in the worst $(1-\alpha)$ fraction of outcomes.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
For the Gaussian portfolio above, $\text{ES}_{0.99} = \frac{\phi(2.326)}{0.01} \times \$1\text{M} \approx 2.665 \times \$1\text{M} = \$2.665\text{M}$, where $\phi$ is the standard normal density. ES is always larger than VaR because it averages over the entire tail beyond the quantile, not just the boundary. 
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
A risk measure $\rho$ is \textbf{coherent} if it satisfies:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \emph{Monotonicity:} $L_1 \leq L_2 \implies \rho(L_1) \leq \rho(L_2)$.
    \item \emph{Translation invariance:} $\rho(L + c) = \rho(L) + c$.
    \item \emph{Positive homogeneity:} $\rho(\lambda L) = \lambda\,\rho(L)$ for $\lambda > 0$.
    \item \emph{Subadditivity:} $\rho(L_1 + L_2) \leq \rho(L_1) + \rho(L_2)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
Expected Shortfall is a coherent risk measure. Value at Risk is not (it violates subadditivity in general).
\end{theorem}

Beyond point-in-time measures, path-dependent risk is captured by drawdowns.

\begin{definition}
Let $W_t$ denote portfolio value. The \textbf{maximum drawdown} over $[0,T]$ is
\begin{align}
    \text{MDD} = \max_{0 \leq t \leq T}\!\Bigl(\sup_{0 \leq s \leq t} W_s - W_t\Bigr).
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Drawdowns measure the largest peak-to-trough decline and capture path-dependent risk that variance-based measures miss entirely. An investor experiencing a 30\% drawdown may be forced to liquidate (margin calls, redemptions) regardless of the portfolio's long-run Sharpe ratio. For this reason, drawdown constraints often dominate allocation decisions in practice: many hedge funds target a maximum drawdown of 10--15\%, which implicitly constrains leverage and concentration far more tightly than a VaR limit.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Diversification and Risk Decomposition}

\begin{proposition}[Diversification]
For a portfolio with weight vector $\vec{w}$ and covariance matrix $\Sigma$,
\begin{align}
    \text{Var}(R_p) = \vec{w}^\top \Sigma\, \vec{w} = \sum_i w_i^2 \sigma_i^2 + \sum_{i \neq j} w_i w_j \rho_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j.
\end{align}
When correlations are imperfect ($|\rho_{ij}| < 1$), the portfolio variance is strictly less than $(\sum_i w_i\sigma_i)^2$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The expansion follows from the definition of the quadratic form. The inequality $\vec{w}^\top\Sigma\,\vec{w} \leq (\sum_i w_i\sigma_i)^2$ holds because the cross terms $w_iw_j\rho_{ij}\sigma_i\sigma_j \leq w_iw_j\sigma_i\sigma_j$ when $\rho_{ij} \leq 1$, with strict inequality when $|\rho_{ij}| < 1$ for at least one pair.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Diversification benefit]
An equal-weight portfolio of $n$ identical assets, each with variance $\sigma^2$ and pairwise correlation $\rho$, has variance
\begin{align}
    \text{Var}(R_p) = \frac{\sigma^2}{n} + \frac{n-1}{n}\rho\sigma^2.
\end{align}
As $n \to \infty$, the variance converges to $\rho\sigma^2$ (the undiversifiable systematic component). For $\sigma = 30\%$ and $\rho = 0.3$: a single asset has variance $9\%$, while a 100-asset portfolio has variance $\approx 2.79\%$ (a 69\% reduction). But the floor $\rho\sigma^2 = 2.70\%$ cannot be breached no matter how many assets are added. Diversification eliminates \textbf{idiosyncratic risk} but not \textbf{systematic risk}.
\end{example}

Understanding where portfolio risk comes from is essential for management.

\begin{definition}
The \textbf{marginal risk contribution} of asset $i$ is
\begin{align}
    \text{MRC}_i = \frac{\partial}{\partial w_i}\text{Var}(R_p) = 2(\Sigma\,\vec{w})_i.
\end{align}
The \textbf{total risk contribution} of asset $i$ is
\begin{align}
    \text{RC}_i = w_i\,(\Sigma\,\vec{w})_i.
\end{align}
These satisfy $\sum_i \text{RC}_i = \vec{w}^\top\Sigma\,\vec{w} = \text{Var}(R_p)$: risk contributions decompose total portfolio variance additively.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
A \textbf{risk parity} portfolio equalizes risk contributions: $\text{RC}_i = \text{RC}_j$ for all $i,j$. This ensures that no single asset dominates the portfolio's risk profile. In a traditional 60/40 stock/bond portfolio, equities contribute roughly 90\% of the total risk despite being only 60\% of the weight, because equity volatility is much higher than bond volatility. Risk parity would increase the bond allocation (often with leverage) until the contributions balance (as in Bridgewater's All Weather fund).
\end{remark}


\subsection{Hedging and Risk Limits}

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{hedge} is a position constructed to reduce sensitivity to a specified risk factor. If a portfolio has factor exposure $\beta_F$ to factor $F$, a hedge with exposure $\beta_h$ satisfying
\begin{align}
    \beta_F + \beta_h = 0
\end{align}
eliminates first-order exposure to $F$.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[Delta hedging an equity portfolio]
A portfolio has $\beta_{\text{MKT}} = 1.3$ (30\% more market-sensitive than the benchmark). To hedge, sell S\&P 500 futures with notional equal to $1.3 \times$ the portfolio value. The hedged portfolio has $\beta_{\text{MKT}} \approx 0$ and retains only idiosyncratic and non-market-factor risk, such that the alpha becomes the primary source of return.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
In practice, hedging reduces expected variance but introduces \textbf{basis risk} (the hedge instrument does not perfectly track the exposure), \textbf{transaction costs} (bid-ask spreads on the hedge), \textbf{roll costs} (if using futures that expire), and \textbf{model risk} (the estimated $\beta$ may be wrong). Perfect hedging is an idealization; practical hedging is a trade-off between residual risk and hedging cost.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{risk limit} constrains a portfolio's risk exposure:
\begin{align}
    \text{VaR}_\alpha(L) \leq \ell_{\max}, \qquad \text{ES}_\alpha(L) \leq e_{\max},
\end{align}
or constraints on factor exposures (e.g., $|\beta_{\text{MKT}}| \leq 0.5$), leverage ($\sum_i|w_i| \leq L_{\max}$), concentration ($|w_i| \leq c_{\max}$), or drawdown.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
If the risk measure $\rho$ is convex (which ES is), then the set $\{\vec{w} : \rho(\vec{w}^\top\vec{R}) \leq c\}$ is convex. Imposing convex risk constraints on the mean-variance problem preserves the convexity of the optimization.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
This is why ES-based constraints are preferable to VaR-based constraints from an optimization standpoint: VaR constraints can produce non-convex feasible sets, leading to multiple local optima and computational difficulties.
\end{remark}



\chapter{Markets and Trading}

The models of the preceding chapters operate in a frictionless world: assets trade continuously, at no cost, in unlimited quantities, at a single well-defined price. Real markets are not like this. Trades execute through limit order books with bid-ask spreads. Large orders move prices, information is asymmetric, and transaction costs erode returns.

\textbf{Market microstructure} studies how these frictions affect price formation, trading behavior, and market quality. This chapter introduces the institutional structure of modern markets (exchanges, order books, market makers), then develops the theory of optimal execution.

\section{Market Structure}

\subsection{Venues and Participants}

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{securities exchange} is a regulated venue where financial instruments are listed and traded. Examples include the NYSE and NASDAQ (equities, options, ETFs), the CME (futures), and ICE (commodities, credit). Securities that are not exchange-traded (forwards and  swaps) trade \textbf{over-the-counter (OTC)}, directly between counterparties or through dealer networks.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Exchange-traded products benefit from central clearing (eliminating bilateral counterparty risk), standardized contracts (enhancing liquidity), and price transparency (all participants see the same order book). OTC products offer customization but require bilateral credit management (hence CVA/XVA, as developed in the preceding chapter). Post-2008 regulation (Dodd-Frank, EMIR) has pushed many OTC derivatives toward central clearing and exchange-like execution.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{broker} is an intermediary that executes trades on behalf of clients, earning a commission or markup. A \textbf{market maker} is a firm that continuously quotes bid and ask prices, standing ready to buy or sell. Market makers provide liquidity and profit from the bid-ask spread, which compensates them for \textbf{adverse selection} (the risk of trading against better-informed counterparties) and \textbf{inventory risk} (the risk of holding unwanted positions).
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Modern electronic market making is a high-frequency, technology-intensive business. Firms like Citadel Securities, Jane Street, and Optiver use sophisticated models to quote prices, manage inventory, and hedge risk across thousands of instruments simultaneously. The bid-ask spread has narrowed dramatically since the advent of electronic trading (from 12.5 cents per share on the NYSE in the 1990s to often less than 1 cent today).
\end{remark}


\subsection{The Limit Order Book}

The central mechanism of modern electronic markets is the limit order book, which aggregates all outstanding buy and sell orders for a given security.

\begin{definition}
A \textbf{central limit order book (CLOB)} records outstanding orders at each price level. At any time $t$, the book consists of:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Bid} (buy) orders at prices $p_1^b > p_2^b > \cdots$ with volumes $q_1^b, q_2^b, \ldots$
    \item \textbf{Ask} (sell) orders at prices $p_1^a < p_2^a < \cdots$ with volumes $q_1^a, q_2^a, \ldots$
\end{enumerate}
The \textbf{best bid} $p_t^b$ and \textbf{best ask} $p_t^a$ define the \textbf{bid-ask spread} $s_t = p_t^a - p_t^b$ and the \textbf{midprice} $m_t = (p_t^a + p_t^b)/2$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
The midprice is commonly used as a proxy for the ``efficient'' or ``true'' price, but this is an approximation. When the order book is asymmetric (more volume on the bid side than the ask side), the efficient price is shifted toward the heavier side (the \textbf{microprice}).
